# Changelog

## 1.11.10

### 🔧 A Super Hopper now feeds furnaces and Super Smelters properly
- **It handed the item in and pulled it straight back out, on the same tick.** A Super Hopper reads from the block above it *and* the ones beside it, and the block it faces is the block it fills - so beside a furnace or a Super Smelter it was the worst case: the coal went into the fuel slot and came back out again, four in and four out, forever, and the machine never burned and never smelted. The block a hopper pushes into is now never one it reads from - a body that both fills and drains one container has a loop, not a route. A Super Hopper that feeds a *chest* beside it is likewise no longer a chest that refills itself.
- **A slot-blind fill put the ore in the fuel slot and the coal in the input.** A furnace is not a chest with three slots: the top face feeds the input, the side feeds the fuel, and the result slot is nobody's to fill - which is exactly how vanilla hoppers behave, because they ask the container which slots a given face exposes and whether it will take the item there. The heavy hoppers now ask the same two questions on every hand-off, so a Super Hopper, a Splitter and a Checker Hopper all feed a furnace or a Super Smelter the way a plain hopper does. A chest, a barrel and a hopper accept everything from every face, so nothing else about them changes.
- Pinned by `hoppers.a-super-hopper-feeds-a-furnace-through-the-right-slot` (from above the ore lands in the input and the output stays empty; from the side the coal lands in the fuel slot and the input stays empty) - **507 checks**.

### 📦 The Bedrock pack is now installable as a Bedrock pack
- **There is a `.mcpack` for Bedrock clients.** The combined `fortuneandfavors-resourcepack.zip` carries both halves - Java `assets/` and `pack.mcmeta` for a Java client, `textures/` and `sounds/` for a Bedrock one - and it is the right file for a Java install and for one download. It is *not* what a Bedrock client imports and not what Geyser's `packs/` folder is given, and a Bedrock client that never receives the pack hears no music and sees no icons whatever the mappings say. `tools/build_resourcepack.py` now writes `fortuneandfavors-bedrock.mcpack` beside it: the Bedrock half alone, no Java half, ready to import or to drop in Geyser's `packs/` folder. This is the likely reason textures *and* music were both vanilla on Bedrock at once - the two halves fail together exactly when the pack itself never lands.
- **The custom-item mapping now hands Geyser a whole number.** `custom_model_data` was written as the Java definition's float (`4550273.0`), and while Geyser's docs describe the value as a float, its own examples write `42` - a JSON `42` satisfies either reader, and a mappings file Geyser refuses to parse is loaded as nothing, leaving every custom item as the vanilla item it is built on. `ffAuditSources` now fails the build if it ever goes back to a non-integer.
- **And the install is written down.** See `BEDROCK-SETUP.md`: the pack (client art and music) and the mappings (what Geyser needs to *send* a custom item) are two separate installs, and the two most common failures - mappings added while Geyser was still running, and a pack that is listed on the client but not activated - look exactly like the pack not working.

### ⚔️ A player killed by a player no longer posts a mob-backup job
- **The board filed a PvP death as if a mob had done it.** `onPlayerDowned` asked only whether the killer was a `LivingEntity` - and a player is one - so a PvP death was recorded with `minecraft:player` as the mob that dropped them, and the board then posted *"Backup: <name> is down - clear 1x Player"*: a dynamic job to kill a creature that does not exist, from an event the hunt board already owns. The killer is now tested for player-ness first, so the one case the code's own comment calls out ("a player kill is deliberately not this") is the one case that cannot reach the board. The kill-spree hunt is untouched and still raises on a 10-kill spree.

## 1.11.9

### 🔥 A sneak-click on a Super Smelter is the tiers window, never a pickup
- **Shift-right-clicking a Super Smelter lifted the machine.** The intercept that opens the tiers window required an **empty hand**, so a player sneaking at their own smelter while holding any item - the ingots they just pulled out, the fuel, the tool they were using - fell through to the generic machine branch and got the machine picked up instead of the window. Sneak-clicking a Super Smelter now always opens `MachineUpgradeMenu`, held item or not, and the generic pickup branch guards against a Super Smelter as a backstop. The window's own PICKUP lore now says it out loud: *a sneak-click opens this window, not a pickup*.

### 💥 An exploded creeper no longer holds its chamber open
- **A creeper that detonated could still count as alive, so the room never opened.** The chamber's roll call asked only whether each tracked body passed `isAlive()` - but a creeper writes its own **`dead` flag the instant it goes off**, *before* the blast, the lingering cloud and the final `discard()` are all done. A body whose discard never landed was therefore left standing at full health with `isAlive() == true` while the game itself already called it dead, and the chamber kept a body on its ledger that no player could ever kill - the doors held shut over an empty room. The ledger now reads the same flag vanilla reads, via `isDeadOrDying()`, for the pack-clear test, the living count, the aura, the strays pull, the glow sweep, the zone sweep and the collapse. A normally-killed body (already `!isAlive()`) is unchanged; a discarded one is unchanged; only the dead-but-present window is now read correctly.
- Pinned by `expedition.an-exploded-creeper-does-not-hold-the-room-open`, which plants the exact state - a real creeper with its `dead` flag set, health untouched and still in the world - and asserts it is gone to the chamber, while a live body is not - **506 checks**.

## 1.11.8

### 🪱 The wormhole takes you to your respawn point, not into your bed
- **`toRespawn` read the respawn *anchor* and teleported onto it.** `getRespawnConfig().respawnData().pos()` is the block the spawn was set *against* - the bed itself, the charged respawn anchor - and being at it is not standing on it: for a bed, vanilla steps the body off the mattress onto the floor beside it, and for a world spawn it finds the first clear pair of blocks. Warping straight onto the anchor dropped the player into the bed (a suffocating, half-block pose), which is what *"tp back to respawn point is broken"* looked like. The warp now runs the game's own search - `findRespawnPositionAndUseSpawnBlock(false, …)`, the same call a real respawn makes - and warps to what it finds; the `false` leaves a charged respawn anchor charged, because a warp is not a death.

### 👾 A Bedrock client is no longer judged on movement it never made
- **The physical movement model now treats a Geyser player the way it already treated them for hitboxes and timing.** Bedrock's own movement - climbing, swimming, vehicles, the way it resolves a step - is *converted* rather than reproduced by the proxy, so a flight arc, a speed window and a jump the server never watched measure the translation layer. The field report was a Bedrock player read as flying and speed-hacking while walking around. `FLIGHT`, `FLIGHT_PREDICTION`, `SPEED`, `SPEED_PREDICTION`, `SIMULATION`, `MOTION`, `AIR_WALK`, `GROUND_SPOOF`, `LONG_JUMP`, `BUNNY_HOP`, `JESUS`, `NO_FALL` and `FAST_CLIMB` join `GEYSER_BLIND`; what the platform does *not* change - ore vision, scaffold, fast-mine, breaking reach - still judges them exactly as anyone else.

### 🕸️ A cobweb is not flight, and the last centimetre of a block is still ground
- **`flight` fired on a body stuck in a cobweb.** A web slows a fall to a crawl, and a crawl is the one vertical delta a hover also produces - so the flight window ("airborne N ticks without losing height") and the fall prediction both read a body in a net as a body holding altitude. A cobweb at the feet or the head now stands the airborne counters down: the web is the world holding the body, which is exactly what they exist to be told. Pinned against the take-back - the same crawl through open air is still flight.
- **`air-walk`/`flight` fired on a body crouched at the edge of a block.** The support probe sampled five points *shrunk two centimetres inward*, so a collision box resting on the last centimetre of a block had every sample land over the void, the world was read as holding nothing, and the edge was flagged. The box is now sampled at its full half-width on nine points (centre, corners and the middle of each edge), so a support smaller than the gap between two corners can no longer fall between them - a block under any part of the footprint is support. The take-back is pinned too: the same pose with the block removed still trips air-walk.
- Pinned by `anticheat.a-bedrock-client-is-not-judged-on-java-geometry` (extended to the movement model), `anticheat.a-cobweb-is-not-flight` and `anticheat.a-crouch-at-the-edge-of-a-block-is-not-flight` - **505 checks**.

## 1.11.7

### 🧑‍✈️ Turning the checks on for operators now lets the failsafe act on them
- **`/ff anticheat op on` measured staff and then let the automatic half stand down, silently.** An operator who ran it to watch a finding land on their own screen got alerts, a ledger entry and a test-mode heartbeat - and then `mayPunish` returned at the top of `enforce()` **without a word**, so a finding that had cleared every other bar looked exactly like a detection that never happened. That is the second reading of *"the warn kick does not kick"*: **"nothing happens"**, from a body whose rank had quietly vetoed the ladder. Either switch is now a yes: `/ff anticheat op on` means the checks measure staff **and** the failsafe may act on them, and `/ff anticheat op kick on` is the same yes for the opposite shape of server - an owner who wants staff kickable while the checks still skip them. A switch that measures a body but leaves the kick switched off is the same silent veto in a smaller box, and it is the reason the ladder read as dead code on a server whose owner was the one testing it.
- **The stand-down is logged, not silent.** The rank gate used to return without a line, so "it detected me and did nothing" and "it never detected me" were indistinguishable in the log. It now names the finding, the level and the command that consents, and `mayPunishRank` is arithmetic over **two** booleans (`staff rank`, `operators consented`) so the rule is still pinnable with no server. `/ff anticheat status` and the status readout print all three states: *checked and kickable*, *exempt but kickable*, *exempt*.
- Pinned by `anticheat.checking-an-operator-is-consent-to-kick-them` (a rank alone is not punishable, either consent switch makes it so, and turning both off puts it back) - **503 checks**.

## 1.11.6

### 🥾 The warn-kick now kicks, and `/ff anticheat clear` works on players who are offline
- **The ladder's first three rungs did not kick.** A *warning* kick is deliberately not a punishment and was filed with no duration at all, so the disconnect it performed was undone by the player reconnecting in the same second - *"warn kick still does not kick"*. It carries a re-entry window now (`AntiCheatStore.WARN_KICK_REENTRY_MILLIS`, **thirty seconds**: half a moderator's, long enough that being removed is felt, short enough that it is still a warning rather than the fourth rung's five minutes), and the join path enforces it. It is filed through a new `AntiCheatStore.warnKick` rather than through `punish`, because `punish` writes any future duration into `timeoutUntil` - the field that means "serving a timeout" - so routing the rung through it would have made the first warning a live five-minute sentence and put the player on the banned screen. `standingPunishment` now answers "not a status" for a warning kick **whatever window it is carrying**, so the rung stays off the banned screen. The hold message also names who removed them: *"The anticheat kicked you (Ns)"* rather than a moderator who did nothing.
- **A warning spent the enforcement cooldown, so the two strikes the player is supposed to see became sixty seconds of silence.** `track.enforcedAt` was stamped when the *decision* was reached rather than when the *action* was taken, and it sits above the alert threshold - so the first passing finding burned the window that is supposed to separate actions, and the kick that should have followed the third alert waited out a minute the player was never told about. The stamp now happens where the action does; the ladder's rungs are read through the existing `mayStrike` predicate instead of a second copy of the same comparison.
- **`/ff anticheat clear <player>` only worked on players who were online.** A body waiting out a kick window or a timeout is by definition *not* on the server - that is what waiting means - so resolving the target through the player list meant the command could only ever work for somebody who did not need it. It now falls back to the stored record by name (case-insensitively, the way the name is typed), clears it by uuid through the same `AntiCheat.clear`, and says which road it took. Deliberately still **not** an unban.
- Pinned by `anticheat.the-warn-kick-now-holds-them-out` (the rung files a window, `kickHoldsFor` sees it, the hold names the anticheat rather than a moderator, the timeout field is untouched, and `/ff anticheat clear` lifts it), `anticheat.clear-works-while-they-are-offline` (a kick window and an advanced ladder are found by name, cleared by uuid, and gone) and the extended `anticheat.a-kick-is-a-punishment-and-a-warning-is-not` (a *live* warning kick is still not a ban) - **503 checks**.

## 1.11.5

### 🛡️ A shield block is not a fast-use hack, and a live dragon is no longer adopted twice a second
- **`fast-use` fired every time you blocked with a shield.** The check records which item is being used, then reads it back at the end of the use to see whether it was *consumed* - and it read that item out of the **main hand**. A shield normally sits in the offhand with a sword in the main, so the read saw the sword: a different item, which the consumption test read as "the used item vanished", and every block was a finding. The item is now read from the hand the use was *started* in, so a shield that is still a shield is never a completion; a real fast use (a stack gone from its own hand) still is. Raised as `useTracking`'s hand tracking plus two named helpers, `AntiCheat.usedInTheHand` and `AntiCheat.useWasConsumed`.
- **`Ender dragon rework: adopted the dragon at … HP` spammed the log every two seconds, and each line was doing real damage.** The search's cache compared the body's level by **object identity**; when that check missed, the field was cleared, the rescan was throttled, and every other sweep re-adopted the *same living body* as if it were a brand-new fight. A fresh adoption is not free - it empties the ledger of who has been paid (`forgetEndLoot`), re-registers the shared boss layer, resets the bar's smoothing and hands back the nova's spent charge - so a fight that never changed was having its books wiped underneath it. Two fixes: the level is compared by **dimension key** (a level instance that wraps the End is not the question), and adoption is keyed on the body's **uuid** rather than on the field's own identity, so a body the current fight already owns is *re-attached* - field restored, nothing reset, no log line - instead of adopted again. (The new uuid is also dropped on a real death and by the harness, so the next body is a new fight.)
- Pinned by `anticheat.a-shield-block-is-not-fast-use` (an offhand use reads back as the shield and is not consumed, the main-hand reading *is* the bug, and an item gone from its own hand is still a completion) and `dragon.the-same-dragon-is-not-adopted-twice` (with the search's memory dropped - the state every miss left behind - the same live body is re-attached to its fight and never counts as a fresh adoption) - **501 checks**.

## 1.11.4

### 🛠️ The anticheat now actually kicks, stops flagging stairs and sprint-jumps, and has a way to clear the wait
- **The automatic half no longer stands down because a moderator is online.** "A person present is a better judge than a threshold" sounded careful and was, on a server that always has an admin connected, simply dead code - the report was *"nobody is ever kicked at all"*. Staff are still alerted to every finding, and whether staff were online is written into the action's log line; the failsafe just no longer waits for them to log off before it does its job. (The operator exemption and `/ff anticheat op kick` are unchanged: your own account still has to consent.)
- **The warn-kick now counts alerts, not a hidden strike counter.** The ladder waits on the number of alerts the ledger already records - the same number the moderation screen shows - rather than on a private counter nothing outside the track could read, so *"how many alerts have there been"* has one answer everywhere. The message the player sees and the removal reason both name the count (`Speed (momentum) (simulation, 3 alerts): ...`), and `ENFORCE_STRIKES` is now an alias of the new `ENFORCE_ALERTS`, so the two can never drift apart.
- **Speed (momentum) no longer fires on stairs.** The momentum model judges a grounded tick against vanilla's flat-ground friction, and a stair is not flat: the client resolves the step by moving the body up and along inside one tick, which the model read as speed gained out of nothing. The surface is now asked rather than guessed at - a full block is judged, a **stair or a slab** is not - so running up a staircase is no longer a finding.
- **Flight (flight-prediction) no longer fires on sprint-jumping.** Holding jump makes the client re-jump on the very tick it touches down, so the packet for that tick is already most of a jump off the floor; the arc stayed open across the whole run and an honest player bouncing along flat ground was told they were flying. The prediction now restarts on the hop's own impulse - any rise the size of a jump - instead of waiting for the world probe to see a landing it structurally cannot see. A body that *climbs under its own power* rises further than a jump through one sample, so it is still judged, and the hover control is untouched.
- **`/ff anticheat clear <player>` stops the wait.** A kick's re-entry window, a live timeout and the automatic ladder are all *waiting* rather than *punished*, so one command lifts them - and deliberately does **not** touch a ban, which is a decision a person made about a different thing. It clears the live strikes too, so an owner who knows a flagged player is clean does not have to restart the server to let them back in.
- Pinned by `anticheat.the-kick-decision-counts-alerts` (the ladder and the alert count are one number, and it takes more than one alert to act), `anticheat.clear-stops-the-wait` (a filed kick window and an advanced ladder are cleared, and the ban predicate is untouched) and `anticheat.a-stair-is-not-a-momentum-hack` (stone is judged; stairs and slabs are not) - plus the existing `anticheat.a-sprint-jump-is-not-flight`, whose three runs (flat ground, the never-seen-at-the-surface run, and a server behind by half) now all stay quiet.

### ⚡ The Item Sorter and Transfer Hopper now move at the Super Hopper's rate
- **They used to trickle eight items a pulse while the Super Hopper moved a whole stack**, which made a sorter fed by a farm slower than the vanilla hopper it replaced. Both now move on the Super Hopper's own budget - `MachineTuning.hopperBudget`, a full stack a pulse at tier one - spent across the pull and the push in one pulse, exactly like `HeavyHoppers.tickSuperHopper`. The Overflow Hopper rides the same engine and inherits it. `/ff help machines` now says so.
- Pinned by `sorter.a-sorter-moves-a-stack-a-pulse` (a whole stack leaves the chest above a sorter in one pulse and reaches its output in two).

## 1.11.3

### 🧯 The Overflow Hopper - fill the chest you point at, and spill only what will not fit
- **A Transfer Hopper empties itself, which is exactly wrong for a chest you want kept topped up.** So there is a variant: the **Overflow Hopper** fills the container it normally points at like a Transfer Hopper, and only the items that *will not fit* it diverts to its output tag - a tagged chest, or another Transfer Hopper. A furnace's fuel chest stays full, an input chest stays stocked, and a farm's surplus is the only thing that travels onward, without a second machine or a filter list to say so.
- **The window says what the machine is doing right now.** Opening it names the container being filled and reads its state live - **FULL** or **HAS ROOM** - so "why is nothing moving" has an answer on screen instead of three hoppers to check. The *Send to* button and the link row speak in spillway wording ("spill", "overflow is off"), because a tag here is a spillway and not a second output.
- **Chaining works the way the Transfer Hopper's does, in reverse order.** The automatic route refuses to hand a stack to another member of the transfer family, so two hoppers cannot pass the same item back and forth; a tag does the chaining, on purpose. Naming another Transfer Hopper (or another Overflow Hopper) as the spillway hands the surplus straight into its buffer, and *it* then fills its own chest first.
- **One shared engine, not a second copy of it.** Both machines run the same tick, with a single flag choosing whether the output tag is tried first (Transfer - take everything out) or last (Overflow - fill first, spill second). An Overflow Hopper walks its whole destination list inside one pulse while it has overflow to place, where a Transfer Hopper stops at its first success. `MachineManager.isTransferFamily` names the pair so the click handler, the source-skip, the heavy-hopper accounting and the VFX all treat them alike.
- Its own held icon (`brim`, a filled crate) and accent `(255, 108, 92)`, a dripping-honey particle, and a **55,000** price on the Exclusive shelf beside the Transfer Hopper it is built from. Item definition threshold `4550359` on the hopper base, so the vanilla fallback keeps the plain hopper's art for anyone without the pack.
- Pinned by `hoppers.an-overflow-hopper-fills-first-then-spills-over` (a full chest in front keeps the stack at home with no spillway named; tag a spare chest and the surplus lands there; empty the front chest and the same stack returns to it, the spillway untouched) and `hoppers.an-overflow-hopper-spills-into-another-transfer-hopper` (the surplus reaches a second hopper's buffer, which then fills its own chest) - **495 checks green**.

## 1.11.2

### 🩹 Eleven held icons were invisible, and the transfer hopper's output is finally one-way
- **The corrupted-icons bug was a wrong vanilla fallback, and it hit eleven base items.** A custom item definition at `assets/minecraft/items/<base>.json` **replaces** vanilla's own definition of that item, so it has to repeat the fallback vanilla used - and the machine-icon generator guessed `minecraft:item/<base>` for every base item. Vanilla mostly uses `minecraft:block/<base>` for *block* items, so every plain **furnace, iron block, gold block, crafting table, smithing table, lodestone, grindstone, blast furnace, smoker, composter and observer** drew as the missing-texture checkerboard, and so did every GUI built on one - starting with the **Super Smelter's tier window**, whose header is a furnace, and the **Item Forge's** smithing table. All thirteen fallbacks are correct now, and the generator **reads vanilla's own definitions out of the client jar** and refuses to run if its table disagrees with them, so the guess can never drift again.
- **A new audit gate makes this class of bug impossible to ship quietly.** `ffAuditSources` knows that a `minecraft:` model or parent that is not in the vanilla client jar renders as the checkerboard, and it walks every item definition and model in the pack against the jar's own model list. It fails the build on a missing reference *and* fails if the jar is absent or carries no models - a check that passes on an empty list is worse than no check. `377 minecraft: model reference(s) in the pack exist in the vanilla client jar`, verified by breaking a fallback on purpose and watching it fail.
- **The Transfer Hopper's output tag is an output and nothing else.** "Send everything to this chest" was quietly also "read from this chest" whenever it stood beside the hopper: the tagged container was still a local source, so the hopper picked its own output up and put it back down - and with the chest full, there was nowhere to write to and the stack it had taken stayed in the hopper. The output tag's container is now struck off the source list before anything is read, **including both halves of a double chest**. A tag is one job or the other, too: naming a tag as the output takes it off the input list, and clicking a tag that is already the output is refused with a line saying how to take it off instead.
- **Two Transfer Hoppers can be chained, on purpose.** Naming another Transfer Hopper as the output hands items straight to it - and the *automatic* route now refuses another transfer hopper, because two of them beside each other were passing the same stack back and forth every pulse: the buffer never settled, nothing arrived anywhere, and both sparkled as if they were working. A chain is a decision, so it is written down. The window, the item's lore and `/ff help machines` all say so.
- Pinned by `hoppers.a-transfer-hoppers-output-tag-is-a-one-way-pipe` (a full output chest keeps every ingot, and the hopper's buffer stays empty - the theft is only visible when there is nowhere to write) and `hoppers.two-transfer-hoppers-are-chained-by-the-output-tag` (no tag, no hand-off; tag it and the stack travels; the second hopper holds it rather than passing it back) - **493 checks green**.

## 1.11.1

### 🎛️ Machine tiers, a smelter that stays fast, held icons, and particles you can switch off
- **The Super Smelter only smelted *one* item fast, and now it does not.** A furnace decides how long a cook takes in two places: `getTotalCookTime`, which is what the input slot asks when an item goes in, and the tick a cook finishes, where `serverTick` writes the recipe's own time straight over the field. Only the first of those was being scaled, so the first item took the fast number and the moment it popped out vanilla replaced it with the recipe's - the machine was a normal furnace from the second item onward. Both writes are scaled now, so **every** item after the first is fast too. Pinned by `smelter.the-second-item-is-fast-too`, which runs a real furnace for 220 ticks with two ores in it; a plain furnace needs 400.
- **Super Hopper and Super Smelter have tiers, bought where the machine stands.** Five tiers each, paid for in cash: a Super Hopper moves 1/2/3/4/5 stacks a tick, and a Super Smelter goes from 2.5x a furnace to about 6.7x - while **burning fuel faster as it speeds up**, so what a player buys is throughput and not a cheaper ingot. Open the window with an empty hand on a Super Hopper, or sneak-right-click a Super Smelter (a plain right-click is its furnace GUI and has to stay that way; the window carries a "pick it up" button so nothing became unreachable). A tier is carried on the item, so picking a machine up and putting it down keeps what was paid for. `machine_tuning.json` keeps the numbers, separate from the machine ledger so a bad tuning write can never cost anyone a machine.
- **Every custom block has its own held icon.** The machines were vanilla blocks with a name - and the vanilla block's *picture*: the Super Hopper in a hand drew the same sprite as the hopper in a farm chest beside it. All 19 machines now have an item sprite of their own, one family (the same casing) with a distinct glyph and colour each, drawn by `tools/make_machine_item_textures.py` and defined on the base item they are minted on.
- **The Item Checker Hopper can be a bin.** An empty-hand right-click switches it between passing its junk on and **destroying** it where it stands - so one hopper in a corner is a trash can, and pointed at a chest it empties that chest of everything your sorters ignore. The setting travels with the item.
- **A tagged container can be the Transfer Hopper's output directly.** The window already had a *Send to* button that stepped through the tags one at a time; now **shift-clicking a tag in that row** makes it the output (and clicking it again takes the name off), which is the same decision made from the tag the player is already looking at.
- **Machine particles, and a real ceiling on them.** Working machines give off a small coloured pulse - a sorter's sparkle, a smelter's flame, the checker's smoke - and a large machine park no longer means a large number of them: **at most five appear in any one tick across the whole server**, each machine waits between its own pulses, and `/ff config machine_particles` (also a switch in `/ff config`) turns the whole thing off. It governs the mod's machine particles and nothing else - vanilla particles, boss telegraphs and every other effect are untouched. Pinned by `machines.tiers-and-the-particle-budget-are-sane` (491 checks green).

## 1.11.0

### 🏭 Five machines that move items how you actually want, and a window that tells you what to do
- **The Item Sorter window now says what to do, step by step.** The layout is unchanged - it was never the shape that was hard, it was the reading of it - so the controls are labelled as a sequence: *Step 1 - Read from*, *Step 2 - Send to*, then the list and the containers below it, with the header spelling out the two clicks (shift-click a pack item to keep it, click a container to send it there). Nothing moved, so nothing a player already learned is wrong.
- **Super Hopper** - a hopper with the throttle removed. Vanilla moves one item every eight ticks; this moves a **whole stack a tick**, above-sucks and same-face-pushes, unfiltered. It is the answer to a farm that backs up at the hopper instead of at the chest.
- **Transfer Hopper** - the sorter's transfer engine with no filter, and tag links on both ends. It pulls from every container around it *and* from any tagged container you link as an input, and hands everything on to the way it faces - or to a tagged container you pick as its **output**. The same window opens, in a transfer shape: no filter row, an input-tag row, and a *Send to* button that steps through the tags in reach.
- **Item Checker Hopper** - keeps only what no placed sorter wants. Point it at a farm's output and it takes the items no Item Sorter's filter names (junk), leaving everything the sorters do want exactly where it is, and passes the junk on. **Facing another Checker Hopper hands the whole stack straight over** - no travel - so a chain of them reaches a far chest or a void without a belt of hoppers between.
- **2-Way Splitter** - pulls from above and hands one item at a time, **alternately**, to the two containers beside it, so a single line becomes two equal ones down to the item. The alternation survives a tick that had nowhere to put anything - the item is not lost and the next side gets the turn.
- **Super Smelter** - a furnace that **cooks everything** a furnace can (ores, food, glass, stone, clay) at a little over twice a furnace's speed - faster than a blast furnace - and **drinks fuel about three times as fast** to do it. Built on an ordinary furnace, so it lights, hoppers and opens exactly like one; the vanilla cook timer and burn clock are the only things adjusted.
- **The Sorter Tag is now sold on the Exclusive shelf** beside the sorter that needs it.
- Pinned by `hoppers.a-super-hopper-moves-a-stack-a-tick`, `hoppers.a-splitter-splits-evenly-two-ways`, `hoppers.a-checker-hopper-takes-only-what-no-sorter-wants`, `hoppers.a-transfer-hopper-moves-everything` and `shop.the-sorter-tag-is-on-the-exclusive-shelf` (488 checks green).

## 1.10.6

### 🏷️ Tagging takes a tool of its own, and a double chest is one chest
- **Only a Sorter Tag names a container now.** The 1.10.5 tag system answered a right-click with *any* vanilla sign, which meant a player who put a sign on a chest to label it had quietly turned that chest into a remote input for every sorter that knew the name. The tag is a **custom item** now - a sign of its own, with its own art so it is not mistaken for a plain one in the hotbar - and an ordinary vanilla sign stays an ordinary sign. Right-click a container with the tag to name it, sneak-right-click to untag, and rename it in an anvil to choose the tag's name; a fresh tag hands out the next `C1`, `C2` rather than claiming its own default name. The tag is not placeable: its whole click is the tag, so it can never be stood in the world as a block by mistake.
- **A double chest is one container to a sorter.** A double chest is two block entities and one container, and a sorter that walked the blocks beside it saw each half separately: it would read the same chest twice on a pulse and only ever fill **half** of it. Every position the sorter reads, pushes to or offers as a destination now resolves through vanilla's own combined container (`ChestBlock.getContainer`), so a pair is the **54 slots** a player sees when they open it. The far half is read, the pair counts **once** in the window's destination list, and the "never hand it straight back" rule covers **both** halves so a sorter cannot stir items between the two sides of the chest it is reading.
- The tag path is deliberately answered *before* the placeholder refusal, because the Sorter Tag is a sign that must never be placed - answering after it would have the tag refused as a ghost block. The `/ff help machines` text and the sorter's window say "Sorter Tag" rather than "sign" wherever they used to.
- Pinned by `sorter.only-the-tag-item-tags-a-container` (a vanilla sign leaves the chest untagged, the Sorter Tag names it, a sneak-click removes it) and `sorter.a-double-chest-is-one-container` (a hand-built pair reads as 54 slots, iron in the far half is pulled while the near half's dirt stays, and the pair is offered as one destination - 483 checks green).

## 1.10.5

### 🏷️ Tag a chest anywhere, and let a sorter pull out of it
- **A sorter can now read a container it is not touching.** An item sorter only ever looked at the six blocks around it, which is the right default and hopeless for the build where the farm's output collects in one chest in another room. **Right-click any container with a sign** and it is tagged; the mod names the tag for you (`C1`, `C2`), or **rename the sign first** and the tag is that name. The sorter's window grew a fifth row listing the tags in reach, and clicking one makes that container an input: the sorter pulls out of it exactly as if it stood beside the block, so **the filter still decides** - tag a chest holding 5 iron and 5 dirt, point a sorter at it with iron in its list, and the iron travels to the sorter's output while the dirt does not move.
- **The tag is the address, not the coordinate.** A sorter stores the *name* it was pointed at, so moving a mark - untag the chest, tag a different one with the same name - moves every sorter that names it. That is the whole reason to write a name down instead of a position: rewiring a line is one click on a chest, and nothing in the sorter's settings changes. A tag whose container is broken is dropped rather than obeyed at a hole, and two containers can never share a name, because a tag that meant two places would silently rewire a base.
- **What it is not: a chunk loader.** A container in an unloaded chunk has no block entity to ask, so a linked sorter finds nothing there until a player or a chunk anchor loads it. The window says so rather than the machine pretending. The reach is **32 blocks**, and the local containers are still asked first - a remote chest is the fallback, and only one stack moves per pulse either way, so the sorter keeps a steady rate instead of draining a remote chest the tick it is wired up.
- **The mod's own signs are not tags.** The buy and sell signs are signs too, so the tag branch is checked only for plain vanilla signs - otherwise "place the shop sign on that chest" would have become "tag that chest", which is exactly the sort of quiet rewiring that keeps a shop from opening. (Caught by `shop.lifecycle` on the first run of the new branch.)
- Pinned by `sorter.a-tagged-container-is-a-remote-input`, which tags a chest ten blocks away, proves an unlinked sorter cannot reach it, links it and checks that the iron arrives and the dirt stays, then moves the mark to another chest and checks the same link followed the name (481 checks green).

## 1.10.4

### 📦 One sorter, several destinations - chosen per item, and never fed by another sorter
- **The list decides where each thing goes, not just what is kept.** An entry can now name the container it wants: the window grew a bottom row of the containers in reach - drawn as the chests, barrels and hoppers they are - and the verbs are two clicks. Click an item in the list to choose it, click a container in the bottom row to send *that item* there. Everything without a name of its own follows the sorter's other output setting (the way it points, the nearest container, or one you pin), so "keep iron and gold, put the iron in the barrel by the door and the gold down the line" is now **one machine** instead of a second sorter and a length of hopper turning the corner. Destinations are written into `sorters.json` per item, and a window that says where each entry goes also says when a destination has been lost.
- **A destination the world has taken away is forgotten, not obeyed forever.** Break the barrel an item was bound to (or lose the claim it stood on) and the sorter drops that one binding instead of holding items nobody can see and stopping the line - which is the difference between a machine that has stopped and a machine that is waiting.
- **A sorter never reads out of another sorter.** A sorter is a hopper, so "the containers beside it" and "hoppers" both looked straight through it: a second sorter in the line would quietly empty the first one's buffer - including the items it was *holding*, the ones whose own destination is full. Feeding a sorter from a chest or a hopper is the build; feeding it out of another sorter is two machines arguing. Pushing **into** another sorter is still allowed, which is how a chain of them routes deeper.
- **The automatic routes stopped stirring.** The rule that a container is never both a source and a destination now belongs to the automatic routes only - a sorter pointed at the chest above it takes an item out and keeps it rather than handing it straight back, which used to look like a machine that is working and moves nothing. An explicit choice - a per-item destination, or the default output pinned at a container - is obeyed wherever the player points it, including at a container the sorter also reads from, because a person made that choice and a text field should not overrule it.
- Pinned by `sorter.a-sorter-is-not-a-chest-to-read-from`, `sorter.each-item-can-be-sent-its-own-way` and `sorter.an-automatic-route-does-not-bounce-back` (480 checks green), alongside the two 1.10.3 checks for the window's clicks and the output settings.

## 1.10.3

### 🔀 The Item Sorter filters the item you pointed at, and hands the rest where you say
- **The window was filtering a different item than the one that was clicked.** The list is the only place a filter is written from, and it read the stack out of the player's pack with `slotId - 27` - but the player's inventory is not in the order it is drawn in. The first slot of the drawn grid is inventory index 9, so a click anywhere in the top row of the pack toggled a **hotbar** slot instead, and the item that was actually clicked was usually still unticked. That is the whole of "I can't put what items it chooses": the click did something, just not to the thing under the cursor. The window reads the slot that was clicked now, and the two verbs are the ones the hand already has - **shift-click a pack item** to add it to the list or take it off, or a plain click, which toggles it *and* lifts the stack on to the cursor exactly as the client already drew it.
- **The fourteenth entry was sitting on the close button.** Close lived at slot 22, which is the fourteenth filter slot: from fourteen entries up, the barrier was overwritten by the entry (so there was no way to close a full sorter) and clicking that entry **closed the window instead of removing it**. Every control now lives in the top row - reading, output, pin, info, empty, close - and the two rows below are the eighteen filter slots and nothing else, which is exactly the list's own cap.
- **The output is a setting, not a guess.** A sorter had one way to hand things on: the face it points at, then up, then around. It now has three answers, cycled from the window - **the way it points** (the vanilla fallback), **the nearest container in reach** (three blocks sideways, two up and down), or **one container you pin**, stepped through the containers it can actually see and remembered in `sorters.json` so a restart does not unpin it. One rule keeps all three from being a loop: **a container the sorter reads from is never a container it puts into**, which is also why the chest above a hopper is offered as a source and never as a destination. Pinned and verified: with the barrel pinned, a filtered diamond leaves the chest above and lands in the barrel - and **nothing** lands in the chest the hopper's own face points at; unpinned, the same diamond goes down the face again.
- **The window stops answering for a machine that is not there.** An empty hand always opens the window - the old sneak-to-cycle-the-reading branch was dead code, because a sneak-click on a machine is read one level up as *pick this machine up* and can never reach it, so the reading is cycled from the window like everything else. If the sorter is broken out from under an open window, the window closes with a sentence instead of quietly creating a settings row for a machine that is not there, and `ItemSorter.forget` drops a position's settings for the paths that are finished with one. `/ff help machines` also lists the eight machines it never mentioned: the sorter, the chunk anchor, the repair station, the two kitchens and the three farm machines.
- **The sorter is silent while it works.** It used to play a pickup pop every other second whenever it moved something - which, on a machine that runs unattended all day, is a metronome in the base - and the `% 40` gate that was meant to keep it rare read a **frozen zero** in every realm (the same frozen clock the 1.10.1 entry is about), so there it played on *every* pulse instead. The sparkle over the block stays, and the click the window makes when you add an item to the list or change a setting is untouched.
- Pinned by `sorter.the-window-filters-the-item-you-clicked` and `sorter.the-output-can-be-aimed` (477 checks green).

## 1.10.2

### 🔒 `/shop` is shut in the cell block, for every client
- **The jail had two doors and only one was locked.** A typed command arrives as one of two packets - the unsigned `ServerboundChatCommand` when the client has no chat session, the signed `ServerboundChatCommandSigned` when it has one - and the prison's guard was hooked to the unsigned handler alone. So `/shop` (and `/gamemode`, and `/tp`) were refused for a client with no chat session and **ran for everyone whose client signs its commands**, which is every online-mode client: the exact case where "no commands in prison" was reported broken. Both guards - the prison's and the duel's, which had the same hole for the same reason - now hang off `tryHandleChat`, the one private method both packets funnel through. The signed path is why that is the *right* chokepoint and not merely a convenient one: vanilla applies the packet's last-seen-messages update at the top of `handleSignedChatCommand`, before the command reaches `tryHandleChat`, so refusing there leaves the client's message chain intact where cancelling the packet outright would have quietly broken its chat.
- **The shop is not a door the block has.** `/shop` was already outside the allowed list, but the refusal sentence lived inside the mixin; it is now `PrisonManager.commandRefusal`, so what a prisoner is told is part of the rule a test can ask. The catalog's pickaxes are the mine's whole point, so `/shop`, `/shop exclusive` and `/buy` are all shut, while `/sell`, `/rankup`, `/prison` and `/ff` are untouched.
- Pinned by `prison.the-shop-does-not-open-in-the-block` (475 checks green) and a new static gate: every command guard must watch `tryHandleChat`, and must not fall back to `handleChatCommand` alone.

## 1.10.1

### ⏱️ One clock for every boss, a perf report you can read a regression off, and a vanish that talks back
- **`/ff perf report` is a command now, and it counts the world as well as the time.** The old `/ff perf` showed only what each instrumented system was spending, which is the wrong half of the question when the server is already struggling: a TPS drop that is *a hundred thousand entities* and one that is *half a million particles a tick* look identical in a cost table. The report leads with the server's own **MSPT/TPS** and the mod's share of it, then every system's **average and spike**, then the **live** block - entities and players per dimension, so a leaking realm is named rather than added up, and **particles per tick with the session peak**. The particle number is fed from a mixin on `ServerLevel.sendParticles`, the one door every particle in the world goes through, so nothing can forget to report itself. `/ff perf reset` drops every sample, so the next report measures only what has happened since - which is the difference between "is this mod expensive" and "is the thing I just changed expensive".
- **The frozen clock is finished.** `level.getGameTime()` does not advance outside the overworld, so a realm fight that scheduled a move against its own level and read it back against the overworld's live clock fired every move on the tick it was scheduled, and a `% N == 0` gate against a frozen zero ran flat out. The last eleven systems - **BossManager**, the **Puppeteer**, the **Time Lord**, the **Clockwork King**, the **Scarlet Devil**, the **Void Shaper**, the **Starbound Magister**, the **Drowned Sovereign**, the **Emerald Sovereign**, the **Gale Warden** and the **Wither** - now run every deadline on the shared `ServerClock`, as do the four places **outside** them that handed a tick in: the devour check in `CombatGear`, the aggressor check in `MobMixin`, the takeover click in `ModEvents`/`LauncherClickMixin`, and the puppet-age seed in the harness. Overworld fights are unchanged in every way that matters: the clock is still monotonic and still 20 a second, it simply no longer depends on which dimension the fight is standing in.
- **`/ff vanish` says what it did.** The command used to be silent in both directions, which is the one thing a stealth mode cannot afford - the operator's only feedback was whether other players could still see them. It now answers in private chat, `/ff vanish on|off` set the state instead of toggling it (so a habit or a script cannot land on the wrong side), and **`/ff vanish status`** lists every hidden body with the diagnostics behind the state: how many times another system cleared a vanish flag, the synced potion-particle count, the emptied slots, and whether the team marker actually landed - which is the difference between "vanish is broken" and "something else on this server is fighting it". The help text also stopped describing a fake disconnect and a fake join line that this vanish has not had for several releases.
- **The textures had three ways to break silently, and all three are build failures now.** None of them was the art: the audit proved every model still resolves its texture. What it could not see was a texture that *exists* and is not an image. The pack now checks that every PNG is a **complete image** - signature, non-zero dimensions, and the trailing `IEND` chunk a truncated file does not have, which is exactly what a half-synced iCloud file is and exactly what draws as the missing-texture checkerboard - and that the **downloadable pack** still carries one entry per texture the art folder has, so a zip that was not rebuilt after an art change can no longer be the reason an item renders as the vanilla thing it is built on. iCloud conflict copies are swept from `resourcepack/` (the art's source of truth, and the one tree the old scrub missed) and skipped by the pack builder, where one landing on an item definition used to make the pack refuse to build at all - with an error about duplicate Bedrock identifiers rather than about the copy.
- Pinned by `clock.a-realm-boss-fight-is-timed-by-the-shared-clock` and `perf.the-report-answers-and-the-particle-counter-is-wired` (474 checks green), plus two new static gates: the eleven boss managers are audited for every frozen-clock read, and the art is audited for images rather than files.

## 1.10.0

### 🌿 The survivability tree, and the timers that never ticked
- **Field Medicine is a tree now, not a line.** It is the root, and **Field Kit** hangs off it - a three-level branch (**60 EXP a level**) bought only after the root, each level answering one of the site's other budget leaks: **venom burns out twice as fast** inside a site, **a fall costs half**, and every **cleared chamber pays fifteen seconds back onto the clock**. The Expedition Shop draws it as a tree rather than a shelf: the root stands in the row of standalone lines and the branch hangs directly beneath it behind a connector pane, and the picture is derived from the same prerequisite the purchase rule reads, so the drawing and the gate cannot disagree.
- **Timers outside the overworld were frozen.** `level.getGameTime()` is only advanced for the overworld - every one of this server's own dimensions, and every other dimension besides, is created with `tickTime` false, so a deadline measured against it was a number that never caught up to itself. The **expedition realm** (collapses, downed windows, guardian beats, entry grace, the run's clock), the **duel realm** (wagers, challenges, every arena schedule), the **End** (the aura, the crystal feed, void rescue, the spins, the dragon's wake) and the **Snow Queen's realm** (her whole rotation, and the seals that hold a player or a mob in the ice) now all run on one shared clock - `ServerClock`, the server's own tick counter plus a base that is persisted, so a deadline also survives a restart.
- **The End intro can be heard through Geyser.** The island's first-visit track is now sent to a Bedrock listener as a music disc the pack redefines (`record.relic`), because Geyser drops a modded sound id it has never heard of - so the one-shot is no longer silence on a Bedrock client, and the stop is recomputed on the same id so the fade is honest too.
- **Both halves of the standalone pack carry the track.** `fortuneandfavors-resourcepack.zip` is rebuilt so *Aria Math* lands in the Java half (`assets/.../sounds.json` and the ogg) and the Bedrock half (`sounds/sound_definitions.json`, plus the `record.relic` alias), and `fortuneandfavors-geyser-mappings.json` is written beside it.
- **The shop's tree is laid out by rule, not by hand.** Position now comes entirely from the `prerequisite` edge - a line's depth down the tree and its column across it are computed by walking the forest - so a branch added later is drawn under its parent with no layout code written for it, and the picture can never disagree with the gate that locks it.
- **The tree explains itself in words.** A new **Survival Tree** guide answers the one question a picture cannot - *why* a line is locked behind another - from a book on the descent window and from the shop itself, and every number it names is read off the shop's own constants so a rebalance cannot leave it describing a shop that no longer exists.
- **The frozen clock was also a quiet TPS leak.** Because `getGameTime()` was frozen at zero outside the overworld, every `% N == 0` gate measured against it was *always* true: the Ender Dragon's aura bit on **every tick** instead of every second, and any other periodic sweep written that way ran flat out. Moving the realms onto one real clock restores those intervals as a side effect of the fix.
- Pinned by `expedition.field-kit-answers-the-other-leaks`, `expedition.the-shop-is-a-tree-and-not-a-shelf`, `expedition.the-guide-explains-the-tree-and-the-gate` and `clock.a-deadline-in-a-realm-advances` (472 checks green).

## 1.9.41

### 🧪 The dungeon breathes, the swarm thins, and the End says hello
- **The health budget was too tight, and there is a line that pays it back.** A site hands back only part of what a mend and a full stomach are worth, and the two cuts read together as "you heal a fifth to a half of what you should". Both were **softened by the same small step** - a mend keeps a little over half of itself and food regen keeps a quarter - and the rest is bought: **Field Medicine**, a new Expedition Shop line (**120 EXP, one level**), hands **fifty points of the penalty back to both** at once. A body with it bought reads the site's reductions as *nothing on a mend* and *a quarter on a meal*, which is the -10%-to-30% the ask named.
- **Poison in a site runs on a short clock.** Venom was the one damage in the maze that did not care what you did: a cave spider lands seven seconds of it on contact, and at the site's thinned budget that is five points of a health you cannot get back. Every source of poison inside a site - the spiders in a wave, a chamber pack, the trapped reliquary - is now **capped at three and a half seconds**, trimmed on the run's own tick so a source added later cannot miss the rule. Amplifier, particles and the HUD icon all survive; only the burn is shorter.
- **The swarms were a stampede.** The last-act avalanche used to be **four plus six at its worst**, arriving behind a body whose health had already been cut, and both of a site event's swarm shapes arrived on top of whatever you were already fighting. The avalanche now **starts at three and stops at seven**, the event waves are all trimmed by one number (`EVENT_SWARM_TRIM`), and the **Deep Mine's wave is no longer a coin toss for a cave spider**: one roll in four is venomous, the rest are spiders, skeletons and zombies. The site's chambers are untouched - this is the wave, not the bestiary.
- **The End says hello once.** A new track - *Aria Math*, the epic cut - now plays the **first time a body walks into the End**, per player, written down in a ledger beside the mod's other data so a restart does not make every visit a first one. It **never loops**, and it **fades out the instant a dragon is on the field**, so the dragon's own theme comes in over a seam rather than a hole; the cue takes the room's music claim and leaves it to the fight rather than handing it back mid-boss-fight. A vanilla client that cannot take the command is served the track as a plain sound instead, because music that arrives and is cut beats music nobody hears.
- Pinned by `expedition.field-medicine-gives-the-health-budget-back`, `expedition.the-site-caps-its-own-venom`, `expedition.the-swarm-is-smaller-and-less-of-it-is-venom` and `dragon.the-island-greets-a-first-visit-once-and-lets-go` (468 checks green).

## 1.9.40

### 🧱 The mine stops growing, and Heat stops evaporating
- **"The prison keeps respawning blocks above the pit."** The refill was one loop with no rules: every two seconds it picked twelve random air cells out of the entire ore slab - **walkway included** - and filled them, near or far, on a body or in a cavern it had just opened out. Blocks appeared under a standing prisoner's feet, behind a digging player's head, and hanging in mid-air over a chamber. It has three rules now, and each one is a mistake the old pass made: the **walk surface is never rebuilt** (only depth one and deeper is touched, so the floor you walk on stays the floor you made), a block is only placed **onto something solid** (so a pass fills a pit from its own bottom up instead of dangling stone over a cavern), and **nothing comes back within six blocks of a body** - so the block that returns is never one you can turn around and watch appear. The rich seam obeys the same clearance rule.
- **Heat cooled off while you read the menu.** A point every 1.5 seconds meant a prisoner could dig at full Heat, stand still for two minutes and be a stranger to the block again - which is not a record, it is a cooldown. The base rate is now **eight seconds a point**, and the cell ladder is what buys it back: every rung is four seconds faster, down to four seconds a point in the Executive Cell. Selling a haul is still the fast way out, and always will be.
- **The uniform is plain leather.** Protection IV on all four pieces made every guard a formality and the manhunt something you could stand still through. The issued kit carries **no enchantments at all** - it is the block's clothing, not a buff - and the audit that pinned it now pins the absence.
- **You cannot build in the cell block.** A prisoner with a stack of cobblestone could roof a guard into a corner, bridge over the pit and wall his own tunnel behind him, and the mine stopped being a place the block made. Placing a block inside the walls is refused; nothing is taken, the stack stays in your hand. Outside the walls you build as you always did.
- **The guards hold you, they do not hit you.** A countdown and an axe landing at the same time is not a minigame, it is a coin flip with a menu over it. While a prisoner is in cuffs, a cell-block guard's blows are **cancelled outright**, so the only thing being timed is the prisoner's own hands.
- **Two seconds a lock, and the cell if you miss it.** The struggle is a reaction test now instead of a nine-second budget: the clock is **per lock** and is refreshed by every lock that opens, so the question is never "can you click five times quickly" but "are you watching the row". Miss it and it is **your own cell** and everything you were carrying - the same sentence the block hands a prisoner it puts down, because both ways to lose in the block should end in the same room.
- **The block says you are on the list.** The Cell Block Guards card now carries a **SEARCHED PRISONER** state: how many sets of cuffs you have broken, that every hit costs five stacks, and - while one is open - a live **RUN WINDOW** countdown, so a prisoner hiding behind a pillar can see how much of their escape is left without staring at the action bar.
- Pinned by `prison.the-refill-never-builds-on-you`, `prison.the-block-is-not-yours-to-build-in`, `prison.a-guard-holds-you-instead-of-hitting-you` and `prison.a-missed-click-ends-in-your-own-cell` (404 checks green).

## 1.9.39

### ⛓️ The capture loop reads at a glance, and the running actually works
- **The struggle was seven identical locks.** The cuff board lit one pane among a row of dark ones, and every pane carried the same little description — so "which do I hit" was a tooltip question, not a look-at-the-screen question. The row has **three readings** now: the lock to click is a **glowing yellow** pane that says so, the locks already worked are **green and ticked**, and the rest are dark and admit they are a slip. The row itself keeps score, and a wrong click visibly takes the green away rather than silently resetting a number in the title.
- **The clock and the step are on the screen.** The info card headlines **`THE CUFFS 2/5`** with the seconds left in red, and the status line names the lock to hit instead of printing a bare counter — `▶ CLICK THE LIT LOCK | lock 2/5 | 4s`.
- **The block now says the rules changed.** Beating your first pair of cuffs used to promote you from "arrest" to "search" quietly, and then your bag started emptying on contact. The block announces it once, as a **title and two chat lines**: every guard hit now costs **five stacks off the top of your bag**, and exactly what you get instead.
- **And the running is a thing that can work.** A guard who steals on every swing empties a bag in six seconds whatever you do, so a taking now buys a **four-second window**: the guard is slowed, you are faster than him, and **the next hit inside the window takes nothing**. Every hit flashes **`SHAKEDOWN -5 stacks | RUN`** and the status line counts the window down, so "sell it, or get off the floor" is a choice you can act on rather than a line you read after the fact.
- Pinned by `prison.the-cuff-locks-read-at-a-glance` and `prison.a-shakedown-gives-you-a-window-to-run` (400 checks green).

## 1.9.38

### ⛓️ The prison, rebuilt around the prisoner
- **The mine was cramped and you could see its roof.** The works are now **33×33 and five blocks of headroom per tier**, twenty blocks of shaft between rungs, under a proper sealed ceiling — so a seam reads as a place you are digging through, not a shelf you can see over. The surface pad moved up and is rebuilt **last**, so a floor can no longer eat the block you arrived on.
- **Broken lanterns, everywhere.** Lanterns that had been *placed as items* littered the mine — knocked out of the ceiling and left as drops. Lights are sea lanterns **set into blocks** now, and a sweep discards any stray lantern item left on the floor, so the mine is lit without raining glass.
- **A round in the Pit now knows its own bodies.** The spawner used to re-sweep the whole arena to decide who was alive, so once you killed the first opponent it read the box as clear and stopped dropping waves. A run keeps a **roll call of the bodies it spawned** and a queued round, and opponents are placed on **distinct gate and corner tiles** instead of all stacking on one tile.
- **"You are still in the area" after you left it.** Arena state was measuring the whole walled apron, so a player standing just outside the fight floor — or the one who had just ranked up — got treated as still in it, and upgrading could drop you **above** the arena. The fight floor and the apron are separate tests now, and upgrading, leaving and being released all rebuild the tier and place you on a fresh, correct floor spawn.
- **The stone you dig comes back.** A tier is regenerated when a prisoner arrives, and again when the last prisoner **leaves** it, so a seam you emptied is full again for the next shift instead of being permanently mined out.
- **The breakout actually works now.** The tunnel is a real **seven-wide, five-tall corridor with a floor and a roof**, the gate bars are mineable with an iron pickaxe — the safety valve the old protected bars never had — and the last bar falling opens the gate. Breaking out **seals the tunnel, scatters the guards, adds Heat and teleports you back to your own floor** instead of replacing the blocks around you while you stand in them. There is a **Warden** who is genuinely re-targeted onto you and re-placed if lost, rather than a guard who never came.
- **Guards no longer spawn in the void.** A guard spot is now searched around the player for a block that is standable — air, air above, **and solid below** — with a fallback ring, so a closing-in guard lands on the ground instead of falling into open air.
- **Captured, cuffed, and shaken down.** The first hit from a guard **cuffs** you and opens a struggle: if you break the cuffs you are free with Speed and Resistance; if you fail you are dropped in **solitary for 25 seconds** and every item you carry is confiscated. Beat the cuffs and the same guard starts **taking five of your most valuable items per hit** — sell or hide, because he is not stopping. Dying in the block now sends you to the **cell**, not the hole.
- **The cell block looks like a cell block.** A corridor with a furnished room per rung, a barred front, a bunk and a window, and death sends you here — and the cell is no longer something you can mine your way out of, because the holding area and the boundary walls are both un-breakable.
- **The uniform.** Every prisoner is issued **Protection IV leather armour named "Prison Uniform"**, confiscated on the way in and taken back on the way out — including the armour and offhand slots, so nothing you walked in with can leak through the walls.
- **X-ray checks stand down inside the walls.** Ore-vision and rush flags are **blind in the prison**, because in a mine *every* wall is an ore wall. Outside, the ground probe now measures the **whole footprint's corners**, so standing at the very edge of a block no longer reads as flight or air-walking.
- Pinned by `prison.the-mine-fits-a-room-and-a-roof`, `prison.the-processing-pad-survives-the-floor-it-sits-on`, `prison.the-uniform-is-issued-and-taken-back`, `prison.a-guard-cuffs-first-then-takes`, `prison.the-hole-is-shaped-like-the-hole`, `prison.guards-are-put-down-on-something-solid`, `anticheat.the-prison-blinds-the-ore-checks` and `anticheat.standing-at-the-edge-of-a-block-is-standing-on-it`.

## 1.9.37

### 🕐 The block has a clock — six timed events, one at a time
- **The prison was the same every time you walked in.** The mine, the pond and the Pit each paid a fixed rate forever, so a prisoner worked out the best loop once and then repeated it. The block now runs **one timed event at a time on a rotation** — four minutes on, three minutes quiet — and every prisoner in every wing is on the same clock.
- **Pit Night** is the headline: **every Pit round pays half again in cash and double in tokens**, and the run's clear bonus doubles with it. Because it is the block's clock and not a personal buff, two prisoners can be mid-run in the Pit at the same time and both be paid the same night.
- **Ore Rush** doubles what the till pays for a legal haul, **Fishing Frenzy** triples every catch at the pond, **Guard Shift** doubles the patrol rate, **Lockdown** makes everything you dig build Heat half again as fast, and **Warden Purge** halves the man's cooldown.
- **Nothing is stored.** The active event is a pure function of the world's game time, so a restart mid-event resumes the same event, every server and every player agree, and no save file can disagree with the clock. The Prison Menu and the Pit board both read out what is running and how long is left.
- Each event announces itself once to the whole block, and only lifts the **one** number it is about — pinned by `events.the-block-runs-one-thing-at-a-time`, `events.each-event-raises-exactly-its-own-number`, `events.every-event-says-what-it-does`, `events.the-clock-comes-back-around` and `events.the-status-line-reads-in-both-phases`.

## 1.9.36

### ⚔️ The Prison Pit — five fights instead of one arena
- **The arena was one fight behind one door.** "Combat Zone" walked you into ten identical waves of unarmed zombies, so the only thing a prisoner ever chose was whether they wanted money - and the answer to *"what fight do you want"* was a number. The floor is now **the Prison Pit**: five fights, picked from a board at the mouth of it, each with its own length, payout and reward.
- **The five fights.** **1v1** — five single opponents, each bigger than the last. **Gauntlet** — ten escalating waves; the old arena, exactly as it was. **Bounty** — three enforcers sent for the block's most wanted head, paying a scale on the block's **hottest Heat record**. **Warden Challenge** — the man himself, once, for real money. **Champion** — this week's named champion, who keeps the name if you can't take it.
- **Fights pay tokens now, not just cash.** Every round pays prison **tokens** on top of its cash, and finishing a run pays a clear bonus - so the Pit feeds the exchange that the mine's contraband feeds, rather than being a separate money tap. A Bounty is priced against the block: **1.0× with nobody hot, up to 1.6× at lockdown.**
- **The weekly champion is a name, not a stat block.** Six champions rotate on a function of the week number, so every server, every restart and every player sees the same one in the same week with nothing stored. Beating them grants the **Pit Champion** title and **three diamond ore** — restricted stone, which means the Pit's best reward is still a bag that has to be carried past a scanner.
- **One round machine, one fact.** The old wave ticker kept *"this round is cleared"* in two parallel maps that could disagree, which is how a wave got paid twice after a restart. A run is one record now - mode, round, and the tick the next one drops - and a round pays exactly once, when the box goes quiet. Walking out of the Pit abandons the run.
- Pinned by `pit.the-five-fights-are-five-different-fights`, `pit.a-bounty-is-priced-against-the-hottest-record`, `pit.the-champion-is-the-weeks-and-rotates`, `pit.the-menu-lists-every-fight`, `pit.a-round-pays-exactly-once` and `pit.the-champion-pays-a-title-and-stone-your-bag-has-to-carry`.

## 1.9.35

### 🏷️ Tags & Titles, and the two "Creator of Mod" badges
- **The mod creator had two badges with one name.** An automatic `Creator of Mod` *tag* that appeared by itself, and an unlocked `Creator of Mod` *title*, with special-case code in three places trying to stop the pair rendering side by side - and a rule that swallowed **every other title** the creator unlocked to keep them apart. The badge is the title now, worn by default: one name, one owner, one row in the menu, and the creator can wear any title they own.
- **The screen says what it does to your name.** The top of Tags & Titles is a **preview of your own name**, drawn with the same position setting and the same one-badge rule the renderer uses, with the two catalogue rows labelled - titles are gold, tags are blue, one of each. "Confusing" was mostly this: nothing on the screen said which catalogue it was looking at.
- **One click, one answer.** Both rows work the same way now: click something you own to wear it, click what you are wearing to take it off. Titles previously answered that click with "keep it", a screen away from tags answering it with "unequip".
- **Nothing is silently dropped.** The title grid has a capacity and used to stop listing at it, which reads as "this title does not exist" rather than "this screen is full". It says so now.
- Pinned by `tags.the-creator-has-one-badge-and-it-is-the-custom-one`.

### 📅 Every day of the streak pays something
- **Eight days out of the ladder paid anything at all** - 1, 3, 7, 14, 21, 30, 60 and 100 - and every other day paid a scaling amount of cash and then told the player "no milestone today". The days a new player is deciding whether the daily is worth opening are exactly the days that were bare.
- **A seven-day shift on top of the cash**, so no day is cheap: **Payday** doubles the day's drop, **Old Scores** pays gems, **Supply Run** pays a Common Mystery Key, and **Week's End** pays a Rare Key and a gem. Milestones still land on top of the shift rather than replacing it, and a milestone key is never worse than the day's shift key.
- **The streak now tells you when you lose it.** A reset to day 1 used to happen silently - the streak simply went back to one. It says what was lost, and what would have carried it.
- **The claim button no longer sits on the calendar.** It was drawn at slot 16, which *is* the seventh calendar card, so the last day of the window was painted over before anyone could read it - which is why the calendar looked a day short. The bottom row has the buttons now, and a **Next Milestone** card says what the seven-day window is working toward.
- Pinned by `streak.every-day-of-the-week-pays-something` and `rewards.the-calendar-is-not-under-the-button`.

### 👋 The first-join screen is a path, not a menu
- Six buttons in one row and a paragraph of prose answered "what is here" and never "what do I do first". It is four rows now: a banner, a **numbered path** from the sell chest to the hub, and a row that only exists to say what the server *contains* - so bosses, duels, guilds, expeditions and the Cell Block are not discovered by accident a week later. The daily step says on its own card whether a reward is already waiting, because the first thing a new player is told to do should not be a dead end.

### 💎 Contraband, the Processing pad, and prison tokens
- **The till buys wages; it does not buy evidence.** Coal, stone, iron, redstone, lapis and raw gold sell where a prisoner stands. **Diamonds, emeralds, netherite scrap and every ore block above them are refused** - which is the point: the risk in the mine was never mining, it was that nothing ever had to be *carried* anywhere.
- **Processing** is a lodestone scale on the intake platform, reachable from the Prison Menu. Standing on it declares the bag. The scanner takes its share first and the payout is what is left: **nothing at CLEAR, a tenth at SUSPICIOUS, a fifth at ALERT, a third under LOCKDOWN, half at CRITICAL**, and it reads the number out *before* it takes anything, so a prisoner can walk away and mine something cheaper instead.
- **A clean declaration is free money, and it is not.** The first cut of this paid the declaration and left the goods in the bag at a zero-percent scan - a printing press with a scan animation on it. Every restricted item leaves the bag either way now, and the two halves always add up to what was carried in.
- **Prison tokens** are earned by declaring hauls (§d1 + $500 of contraband§7) and never by selling at the till, so they are the one payout that reaches no other part of the economy. They are spent in the Prison Menu: **10 tokens → a gem, 25 tokens → a Common Mystery Key.**
- Pinned by `prison.contraband-has-to-be-carried-out`.

## 1.9.34

### 🏭 The prison is a place, not a level number
- **The old ladder was eight anonymous floors separated by ordinary stone**, so the rock between two of them was *income* and the gate between them was optional: dig down far enough and you were in a better mine without ever having ranked into it. The block did not control its own progression; the map did.
- **Six sectors replace it**: the **Yard**, the **Quarry**, the **Furnace**, **Containment**, the **Deep Cell**, and **Maximum Security** - six named places over the eight physical floors, since two galleries is what makes a place a place. You arrive in the Yard and you leave through the top.
- **Nothing outside a sector's own seam is mineable.** The band under the seam, the four walls and the ceiling are the block's own works, refused by the same funnel that refuses a duel arena's map - so the answer to digging at them is a sentence and not a ghost block. **🚨 SECURITY BLOCK - this area is restricted**, with a pointer to the sector you are working toward. The rock between two sectors is protected for its entire thickness, so there is no dig that reaches a place you have not ranked into, and no shape of tunnel that goes around the gate.
- The old answer was three rows of bedrock, which is the one material in the game that says "the map ran out" - it told a prisoner about the world's edges and nothing about the prison. It is the block's own deepslate now, reinforced where a prisoner can see it, and the refusal is the wall.
- **Depth is a decision, not a present.** The same rich block is worth more down there and costs more Heat to take - 0.7× in the Yard, 2.6× in Maximum Security - so the deeper sectors are the ones a prisoner has to be able to *afford to be caught in*.
- Pinned by `prison.the-works-are-not-yours-to-mine`.

### 🌡️ Heat is a situation, with a name
- **A bar and a number told a prisoner at 44 and a prisoner at 46 the same story**, and the guard that arrives at 45 was therefore a surprise every single time. Heat now has five tiers that say what the prison is *doing about you*: **🟢 CLEAR / 🟡 SUSPICIOUS 25 / 🟠 ALERT 45 / 🔴 LOCKDOWN 75 / ☠️ CRITICAL 95**.
- The thresholds are the constants the block already acts on - ALERT begins exactly where the guards do, LOCKDOWN exactly where the Warden does - so the name and the behaviour cannot drift apart, and the two rungs that had no voice of their own (the first time the block starts paying attention, and the point where it stops sending guards and starts sending the man) now speak.
- The menu's Heat tile lists the whole ladder with your rung marked, says what your sector multiplies Heat by, and says how fast your own cell bleeds it off.
- Pinned by `prison.the-heat-has-a-name-and-a-next-thing`.

### 🛏️ A cell is somewhere to lie low
- **The other half of "Heat decreases from staying in your cell" - there was nowhere legal to go**, so the only way to run a record down was to sell a haul. A cell is the one purchase in the block that is about what happens when you *stop*, and what it buys is time.
- Five rungs - **Tiny Cell, Improved Cell, Better Cell, Secure Cell, Executive Cell** - bought out of prison cash. Each rung cools Heat a point faster: **every 1.5s at the bottom of the ladder, every 0.5s at the top**. The baseline rung is the exact rate the tick loop always used, so nobody's Heat behaviour changed underneath them.
- The cooling rate has one owner now - the tick loop reads the cell's number rather than carrying its own - because a rate that can be bought and not delivered is worse than no ladder at all. The tile is in the Prison Menu next to your Heat, where the question it answers is asked.
- Pinned by `prison.a-cell-is-somewhere-to-lie-low`.

## 1.9.33

### 🌬️ The Gale Warden can throw you, but he cannot keep you
- **Every launcher in that fight is a horizontal move with an upward garnish**, and uncapped the garnishes add up: two close together put a body twenty blocks up with its own arc, a third arrives before it has landed, and what a player experiences is a boss with no way back to the floor rather than a boss that throws them about.
- **One rule with one owner.** A single lift is capped at **1.2**, and once a body has been off the ground for **three seconds** the garnish pays nothing at all and whatever climb it still has is bled off - gravity gets the body back every time, whatever the boss is in the middle of. It tapers over the last second rather than stopping dead, so the ladder of launches reads as a fight pressing and then letting go. **Horizontal impulse is untouched**: the fight is still about momentum, and a body thrown flat across the room lands on its own.
- The ceiling lives in the tick loop rather than in the moves, because fourteen call sites are fourteen chances to forget.
- Pinned by `gale.the-ladder-of-lifts-has-a-ceiling`.

### 🚨 A boss throwing you is not a client lying about where it is
- **The constant-correction rule fired on players a hurricane was holding in the air.** A boss that writes a body's motion every tick stands the movement checks down for as long as it lasts, and twelve seconds of that was a finding - on a player who was doing nothing but being thrown around.
- **A refusal is the evidence the rule was always describing**: this module telling a client to take a position it did not claim. So the finding now needs *both* halves - six unbroken seconds of server-driven movement **and ten refusals inside that stretch**. A boss performs none; a client that keeps arriving somewhere impossible performs nothing else.
