- **A Crystal Cavern is a geode now, not a purple box.** Beds of amethyst line the floor with small, medium and large buds and clusters standing on them at every stage of growth, sixteen budding seams hang in the roof, fourteen veins break through the walls, and one spire nobody has dug out stands in a corner - all of it in ordinary tuff, calcite and basalt rock.
- The other sites got the same treatment: mushrooms and half again as many webs in a monster cave, timbered haul roads in a deep mine, standing obsidian stones under soul lanterns in a void hall, bone sarcophagi under snow in a frozen crypt, and anvils on chains with quench cauldrons in a magma forge.

## 1.9.17

### 🎒 Rich chests can hold a Pack Upgrader
- **A new find: the Pack Upgrader.** The rich chests - the ones marked §6§lRich Loot Chest§r - occasionally hold a patch that is stitched into the pack you are carrying, and the pack holds **nine more pieces** for the rest of the run. Click it in the chest and it goes straight onto the pack; right-click one you are holding and it does the same thing. **Three patches is the ceiling, 36 pieces.**
- **It is a rare chest's find, not a common one's.** Plain chests never hold one (`0.0` by test), rich chests start at 18% and get likelier the deeper the run has gone - 23% past chamber ten, 28% past twenty, capped at 35% - and a patch is never offered to a pack that has already taken all three, so a find that could do nothing is never shown.
- **It is the one thing in a chest that is not money.** No price tag, no cash credit: the patch is spent on the pack, and the loot you were already carrying is untouched. A pack at its ceiling leaves the patch in the chest rather than swallowing it.

### 📐 The pack window is as tall as the pack
- **The window grows a row per patch.** Nine pieces opens two rows, a patched pack opens three, four or five, with the label and the close button moving to whichever row is last. A capacity the menu could not display would be a find the player is told about and cannot use.
- The pack's tooltip now reads `Holds 4/18` and adds `Patched x1 " holds 18 pieces instead of 9`, the action bar's `Pack n/18` follows it, and the chest window's label spells out **everything you are already carrying** with its worth - which is the comparison the whole window exists to make, and one a single bottom row stops being enough for once the pack has been patched.

## 1.9.16

### 📈 The Exchange is priced off the server's wallets, not off constants
- **Every shelf is now a share of the benchmark.** The board used to be eleven fixed prices that went stale exactly where it mattered - a $900 share is a position for a player with $10,000 and a rounding error for one with $10,000,000. Each listing now sits on a shelf aimed at a slice of the 90th-percentile wallet: **penny 0.5%, standard 2%, premium 10%, elite 25%**, with a log-symmetric band (`x0.40` to `x2.50`) it cannot leave. Double the server's wallets and every shelf doubles with it.
- **The whole board was rebased onto those targets.** Penny shares are now $1,150-$1,450 rather than $700-$800, standard $3,600-$8,600, premium $19,000-$38,000 and the elite two $80,000/$120,000 instead of $300,000/$450,000 - so the top of the board is a position a mid-server player can actually reach for, and it lifts with the economy instead of being unreachable forever.
- **The 10th/50th/90th percentiles, the richest and the poorest wallets are all on the Market Scale tile**, the level is printed beside them, and the shelf table shows each target and band at the level the board is trading at. The tile's name itself is the benchmark.
- Recalculation is still gradual (a quarter of the gap per fifteen minutes, never more than 20% in one update), still clamped (`x0.15` to `x25`), and the penny shelf is still capped against the **median** wallet, so a new player always has something they can buy.

### 🔁 What you do moves the price
- **Trades leave a mark.** Buying pushes a listing's quote up and selling pushes it down, scaled by how big the order is *against that listing* rather than by share count - a thousand shares of a penny stock and a thousand of an elite one are very different events. The mark fades to nothing over the next few quotes (three quarters of it per quote), is capped at `+42%/-30%`, and **is written to `market.json`**, so a price somebody pushed up survives a restart.
- **The chart does not move.** The clock still sets what a share is worth; players only set what it costs at the till. The windows report the two effects separately: "Market scale: -12%" and "Order flow: +8% buyers are in it".
- The board and every card now print each move as **both** a percentage and money.

### 🖥️ A real order ticket
- **The chart window has a quantity selector.** Step 1 -> 2 -> 5 -> 10 -> 25 -> ... -> 10,000 with the arrows (right-click one to snap to the most you can afford), and the tile in the middle shows the chosen size with its **estimated total cost** and **estimated proceeds**, what you can afford and what you hold.
- **Buy and sell follow the selector**, and refuse politely (with the reason in the tile) when the size does not fit. "Sell everything" still closes the position in one click. The board and the ticket also show each listing's shelf target, its band at the current level, and its risk.

## 1.9.15

### 🚪 You arrive standing up, not inside the floor
- **The expedition entrance pad no longer buries you.** A pad's anchor is the FLOOR block at its centre - the block you walk on - and the arrival teleport used that block's own Y, so a run began with the player *in* the floor. The threshold also stood a four-tall sea-lantern column on the exact arrival point, which is the other half of "I spawn in the ground". The pad's middle is now clear air, its light lives on lantern masts along the gold rim, and `standY` puts every arrival - entrance, void rescue and the Vault Guardian - one block above the floor, dead centre.

### 🏛 Twenty-five kinds of chamber
- **Ten new faces.** The **Sunken Library** (shelf aisles and a lectern dais), the **Armoury** (a rack wall under iron bars, anvils and smithing benches), the **Oubliette** (two rows of barred cells that all open onto the hall), the **Ruined Larder** (barrel shelves and hanging stores), the **Sunken Garden** (moss, a water channel, a grove that outgrew it), the **Fungal Grotto** (mushroom caps that light themselves), the **Forge Hall** (a bricked lava crucible you can be pushed into), the **Broken Crossing** (the floor fell in wall to wall, and the way over is wreckage), the **Gallery of Echoes** (a colonnade of pedestals) and the **Wager Vault** (a coin floor and a bet).
- **Every face has its own pack.** An oubliette wakes gaolers, a forge hall wakes blazes and magma cubes, a garden wakes spiders - a room's face decides who is in it before the dungeon's roster does.

### 🖤 The pack wears a black glow
- **Every body in a chamber glows - in black.** A glowing entity's outline takes its colour from its team, so the pack joins a team whose colour is `BLACK`: a real outline every client already draws, tinted to a silhouette. A thin dark ink aura follows each living body of the chamber you are fighting in, so nothing in a coffered hall is ever just a shape you did not notice.
- The team is emptied at the end of a run and rebuilt when it fills, so a hundred-chamber maze cannot grow the scoreboard forever.

### 🎒 Chests hand over pieces, and you carry nine of them
- **Chests no longer pay cash.** Opening one shows the pieces it holds - rolled for this dungeon at the depth the run has reached, from the dungeon's own trade up to netherite scrap and diamond blocks past chamber ten - and you take them one at a time into a **Loot Backpack**. Nine pieces, no more. A piece you cannot fit stays in the chest for your next visit.
- **The pack is the run's decision.** Right-click it to open it and click a piece to **throw it away** - you lose its worth and gain the space, which is exactly the trade a rich chest asks of you at four pieces deep. Its tooltip lists everything it holds and what the lot is worth carried out.
- **The pack is given on arrival and taken back on exit** (or on death), and any pack found on a player who is not on a run is swept back by the site.
- The old per-run chest cap still applies, but it now gates *taking* a piece rather than paying cash: the window says so instead of quietly eating it.

### 🧭 A Return Compass, and a clock you can buy back
- **The entrance hands you a Return Compass** - a real lodestone compass locked onto the lodestone beside the entrance pad, so the needle points home instead of spinning. It is handed back when you extract (or when the site takes you).
- **Bosses buy time.** A floor guardian's fall adds **3 minutes**, a mini-boss **1 minute**, and the supplier sells **Time Shards** (right-click for **+45 seconds**). Depth is only worth chasing if the thing guarding it pays the clock back.
- **The clock is announced in stages** - half gone, three quarters gone, the last tenth - and in the last tenth the site starts sending waves after you. It is pressure, never a surprise: the walk home is a decision made at half time.

### 💰 The supplier prices its own shelf
- **Twenty-one lines of stock**, from rations in the shallow end to diamond kit, a netherite ingot, an enchanted golden apple and two grades of Time Shard at the deep end.
- **Prices are set at the counter.** Each line gets dearer every time this run buys it, everything gets dearer as the run's secured loot grows, and the whole board drifts on a two-minute **tide** the window reports - cheap, level or dear, in percent.

### 🎲 More to do than fight
- **Interactables, by exact position.** The maze now plants things that answer a right-click: a **Wager Pedestal** (stake $2,000 of secured loot on an honest coin), a **Sealed Reliquary** (usually money, sometimes poison and a pack), a **Rune Lock** (light every rune stone in the chamber and the vault behind them opens for $6,000) and a **Supply Crate** (a heal, bread and torches for the road on).
- **Loot Goblins.** A new site event spawns a gold-crowned, speed-boosted goblin that is worth **$9,000** to catch - the one bounty in the maze you chase instead of clearing.

## 1.9.14

### 👹 Floor guardians: every tenth chamber is the dungeon's boss gate
- **A named boss owns every tenth chamber of depth.** Walk into depth 10, 20, 30... and the room is the **Floor Guardian's Arena** - a lit fighting pit with a trimmed medallion underfoot, a dais, and a ring of lantern-crowned pillars. The doors seal and **the floor guardian wakes** with a retinue: a named, netherite-clad boss at five times a mini-boss's health (`Stonebreaker, Warden of the Deep`, `The Bone Tyrant`, `The Crystal Maw`, `Slagheart, Warden of the Forge` and friends - one legend per dungeon) plus adds scaled by the floor number. The action bar says **GUARDIAN** while the fight runs, and counts the chambers to the next one the rest of the time.
- **His bounty is huge by design** - $25,000 + $2,000 per chamber of depth, times the dungeon's multiplier: a floor-10 guardian in the Boss Lair is worth $100,000 secured. He also mends you to full on his fall, because that is the deal every dungeon crawler makes.
- **...and he drops the Descent Ladder.** Right-click it and the shaft swallows you whole, landing you **five chambers deeper than any door on the floor would take you** - a fresh chamber, carved for you alone, already at the deeper payout scale. It is the one shortcut in the maze, and it costs a boss fight.

### 📰 The front page: when the Exchange shouts
- **A portfolio that swings 20% or more in a day runs above the fold.** The paper keeps yesterday's snapshot of every portfolio and compares: the day's biggest mover is named on the **FRONT PAGE** with the swing, the value and a line from the desk - "Somebody read this morning's tape very, very well" or "A bold position, held boldly, right over the edge."
- **The Exchange's first millionaire is history, and the paper says so - once, ever.** The first trader whose share holdings cross $1,000,000 gets a named splash the desk can never run again, and the record is kept in `newspaper.json` so no second paper can claim the moment twice.
- Both stories are guarded: if the tape cannot be read, the front page says so and the rest of the paper prints as normal.

### 📖 The Expedition Codex
- **A new window off the Expeditions menu** that remembers what this world's explorers have walked, cleared and carried home - across every run, forever (`expedition_codex.json`).
- **Every dungeon has a page**: runs begun, escapes, deepest chamber reached, best single payout, total carried home. Untouched dungeons sit grey until somebody writes their first line.
- **Every chamber has a page too** - all fourteen faces of the maze, from the Combat Pit to the Floor Guardian's Arena: how often it was walked into, how often it was beaten. The header totals the world: runs, escapes, falls, chambers cleared, guardians felled, descents used and the money that made it out.

### 🛠 Expedition polish
- **A cleared chamber mends you.** Four hearts back on every chamber clear, full on a guardian's fall - the dungeon-crawler's after-fight breath.
- **The run scorecard counts the run**: guardians felled and descents used now sit beside the loot, the kills and the depth.
- The floor guardian shares his body with the dungeon's sentinel (one chooser for both), so a new dungeon declares its boss once.

### 🏛 The chambers are rooms now, and nothing locks you in
- **Every archway stays open.** A chamber's mouths used to seal the moment its pack woke - iron bars across all four of them, and because a barred archway barred its neighbour too, walking into a fight barred the doorway you had just walked through. The chamber you were standing in stopped being a room with three ways on and became a box with one pack in it; the only way out of the room was to finish it. Nothing bars a door now, and the floor guardian's arena is a fight you may walk around on your way to something else.
- **The halls are sized like rooms, not like warehouses.** Thirty-four across and twelve tall, with a single lantern cluster over the middle, is a floor you cross for ten seconds with every wall of it in the dark. Chambers are **28x28 and ten tall**, and the interior is dressed rather than merely carved out.
- **They are lit.** A **coffered ceiling** crosses every hall: beams every six paces, a **lantern in the middle of every panel** - sixteen lamps spread across the floor rather than four over the dais - and a **lamp on every pilaster** four blocks up the walls. The Boss Lair's ceiling lamps were unlit redstone lamps, which emit nothing at all, so its halls had no working lights in them; they are lit now.
- **They are furnished.** Baseboard and cornice courses, pilasters sharing the ceiling's grid, a dressed lintel over every archway, an inlaid border ring in every floor, and crates under a hoist chain in each corner. A chamber's own face still paints itself over the top of all of it.
- **A doorway is never furnished shut.** Everything that dresses a hall asks first whether the block it is placing stands in a doorway's walk lane, and every new archway sweeps the three blocks behind its mouth on both sides - the old maze would leave a treasure vault with a solid block in the mouth of the door it had just opened, and an ore vault's wall studs in the way of an arch.

## 1.9.13

### 🚪 Expeditions are a dungeon crawler now
- **A room, a pack, and three doors.** Expeditions are no longer backrooms corridors - they are a maze of **giant chambers** in an infinite grid. Walk into a chamber and the archways seal behind you with iron-bar portcullises; its pack wakes and comes for you. Slay every one of them and the bars fall, a **chamber bounty** lands in your secured loot, and up to **three doors groan open** onto chambers nobody has walked yet. Choose one. The maze never ends, the depth counter only goes up, and every chamber deeper than the last pays more than the last - walking out is always the hard choice.
- **Every chamber is 22x22 and eight tall, with dressed stone to match.** Corner columns, a lantern cluster over the middle of every hall, trim lintels over every archway, and each dungeon's own stone: deepslate underfoot in the Deep Mine, blue ice in the Frozen Crypt, prismarine in the Sunken Temple. The rooms are giants now - you can see the whole fight before it starts.
- **Fourteen kinds of chamber.** The Threshold, Combat Pit, Grand Hall (roof raised three blocks, pillar ring, dais treasure), Treasure Vault (precious-stone floor, chandeliers, two or three rich chests), Trap Floor (plates over hidden TNT, rich chest for the survivor), Ore Vault, Sanctuary (no pack ever wakes here - it mends you), Fountain Court, Mob Den, Pillar Hall, Smuggler's Cache, and three faces that are new this version: the **Forgotten Shrine** (clearing it grants Regeneration, Resistance and an offering), the **Drowned Hall** (rimmed pools over a prismarine floor) and the **Web Nest** (cobwebs everywhere and spiders in every one of them).
- **The room is the lock and the monsters are the key.** A loot chest in an uncleared chamber stays sealed until the pack is dead - so the treasure is always worth the fight, and the fight is always between you and the loot.
- **Everything pays more.** Chest ranges roughly doubled (Deep Mine $900-$2.4k up to Boss Lair $3.5k-$9k), the per-run chest cap went **$25k to $100k**, kill rewards are up half again, and every cleared chamber pays a depth-scaling bounty on top: **$600 + $300 per chamber of depth** times the dungeon's multiplier, with treasure rooms and grand halls paying 1.6x. Going deeper is now a real strategy, not a walk.
- **Three new dungeons.** The **Sunken Temple** (drowned halls of prismarine, drowned and guardians, the Tidecaller waits), the **Frozen Crypt** (blue-ice sepulchers, strays, Grave Chill) and the **Magma Forge** (blackstone foundry over a magma sea, blazes and magma cubes, the Forge Tyrant). Eight dungeons in all, each with its own stone, ores, monsters, den spawner, sentinel name and treasure scale. Every dungeon site is spaced 3,000 blocks apart in the expedition realm, because an infinite maze grows and two runs must never carve into each other.
- **The run scorecard counts the run now**: chambers cleared and the deepest chamber reached, beside the loot, the kills, the ores and the medal. The action bar says which chamber you are standing in, at what depth, how many of the pack still stand, and which doors are sealed.

### 📰 The paper reports the day's market in its own voice
- **EXCHANGE CLOSE, once a day, in the newspaper's voice.** Where the index closed and its move on the day - with the desk's read on it, from "a proper rally - there was not an empty chair on the floor" to "an ugly close - somebody's ledger is weeping tonight" - then **the day's biggest gainer and the day's worst**, each with its real ticker, its real move and a line only a newspaper would print. The Exchange itself still never speaks: no ticker, no alerts - this is the one place the tape gets read out loud.
- **THE FORTUNE BOARD: the richest traders on the Exchange.** Every player holding shares is ranked by what their portfolio is worth right now, with what the position has made them beside it (+14.2% or `new money`), and the board closes with how many traders hold how much in shares between them. Old money gets named; the desk respects a good portfolio.
- Both sections are guarded: if the tape is unreadable the paper still prints, with the line that says why it could not.

## 1.9.12

### 📈 The Exchange - a stock market inside the bank
- **Eight listings, a new quote every five real minutes.** Emerald Exchange, Redstone Foundry, Diamond Consortium, Quartz Optics, Ancient Debris Ltd., Gilded Carrot Farms, Ender Pearl Freight and Blaze Rod Energy - a fat slow blue chip, two middles, and two cheap wild ones. Open it from **`/stocks`**, from the Bank's new **Stocks** tile, or from the hub's Stocks button.
- **The market runs on the world clock, not on the server.** A price is a pure function of *(world salt, listing, the five-minute cell of the epoch)* - there is no running total, nothing to tick and nothing to catch up. That is what makes "it keeps moving while nobody is online" true by construction rather than by a loop nobody can verify: stop the server for a week and every price waiting when it comes back is the price the same grid printed all along, and a chart drawn over the days you were away is the chart those days actually had.
- **A per-world salt, kept in `market.json`.** It is minted once and never rotates, so a world's series is stable - while a player cannot compute tomorrow's price by reading the mod, which is the one thing a deterministic series would otherwise hand over.
- **One market, eight listings.** Every quote feels a shared market factor through its own beta, plus its own noise: beta 0.6 for Ancient Debris, 1.6 for Blaze Rod Energy. So there are rally days when the board is green and ugly days when all of it is red, and the cheap listings swing while the expensive ones drift. Prices are bounded around each listing's base and continuous quote to quote - measured, not assumed: a week of quotes stays inside `0.3x-3x` of base, the worst single-quote move in a year of samples is 4.3%, and the median quote moves 0.2-0.6%.
- **The full-width chart window.** Click a listing and the top four rows of the window are the price: **nine columns of glass panes, oldest left**, green when a quote is above the one before it and red when it is not, the bottom cell of every column always filled and the field above it dark grid. The newest column carries an emerald or a redstone marker on its top cell - the quote the buy and sell buttons are about to use. The compass button steps the window through **45 minutes, 3 hours, a day and a week**, and the figures beside it always say which scale the picture is on and what the low and high were.
- **Buy 1, buy 10, buy as many as you own the money for; sell 1, sell 10, sell everything.** Funding comes from your **wallet first and your vault second**, proceeds land in your wallet, and every trade is written on your bank statement as a Market buy or Market sale. Brokerage is **1% each way**, charged in the player's favour on the rounding - costs round up, proceeds round down.
- **Your average cost is tracked, so the tiles tell the truth.** "Worth $2,400 (cost $2,100) · +$300" is a real figure including the day you sold half, because the basis leaves with the shares that carried it.
- **Silent, on purpose.** Nothing about the market is ever announced in chat - no alert, no ticker, no "EMRLD is up 4%". A share price is something you go and look at, and the only line this system ever writes is the receipt for a button you pressed. An audit rule fails the build if a broadcast ever appears in it.
- **The chart updates itself.** A window left open through the five-minute rollover redraws on its own rather than waiting for its owner to click something, so a live quote never looks like a dead window.

### 🏦 The vault's interest now advances while the server is off
- **Interest used to pay one day for a week away.** The payout tested "is this account overdue" and then paid a single day and stamped it to now, so a world that sat for a week paid a day and quietly swallowed six - and an empty vault that was overdue had its stamp moved forward, so the day was lost rather than deferred. The payout now settles through a pure `catchUp(last, now)`: **every day the vault waited is paid, in order, compounding exactly as before.**
- **Bounded on purpose, at 30 days.** A vault left for a year is paid for a month - interest is a reason to keep cash banked rather than carried, not a substitute for playing. Past the cap the excess is forgiven and the stamp lands on the present, so the next tick cannot find the account still overdue and pay the cap again; the test drives both ends (three days away pays three, a year away pays thirty and then nothing).
- **The payout phase is kept.** A vault that has been paying at six in the evening keeps paying at six in the evening, however long the absence.
- **And the first deposit no longer pays instantly.** A fresh account has "never been paid", which used to read as "a very long time ago" - so the first deposit was followed by a full day's interest on the next tick. A never-paid account now starts its clock instead.
- **The bank's own window says so.** The interest tile shows the catch-up, and the header now carries your stock portfolio beside the vaults, so the bank is one window onto everything you own.

## 1.9.11

### ⛏ Rich ores are counted by the minute now, not by the face
- **A different kind of x-ray needs a different kind of answer.** The three tests already here ask *how* an ore was found - a buried ore with no open face, a yield no seam produces, five finds with nothing dug in between. There is a fourth kind of client, and it beats all three: it reads the world through stone and then mines the ores it can already see, walking to them and digging normally. Every one of those three tests passes for it, because every tunnel it breaks has a face its own dig opened.
- **What it cannot fake is the arrival rate.** There are not thirty diamonds within five minutes of somebody who has to find them by looking. So the count is per ore family and per window: **30 diamonds inside five minutes**, or **10 ancient debris inside the same** - two families rather than one number, because debris is a different ore in a different dimension at a genuinely different honest rate. Counted in **blocks broken**, not items picked up, so Fortune cannot make an efficient miner's counter say ten where their day said three.
- **And a total is not a rate.** This was the explicit ask, and it is the whole of the window: anything older than five minutes is dropped *before* it is counted, so thirty diamonds across an afternoon never reaches the figure and the counter cannot creep up on it. The window is then spent by the report - the same discipline the click and aim windows use - so one rush is one finding rather than one per ore.
- **Staff are told and the miner is told nothing.** No message, no correction, no kick: the check is a rate rather than a physical impossibility, so it is absent from the auto-enforce list, and an audit rule fails the build if a line of chat ever appears in that method. Pinned by `anticheat.a-rush-is-a-rate-not-a-total`, which drives the real window arithmetic in both directions - thirty diamonds five seconds apart reports, and five hundred diamonds two minutes apart, seventeen hours of mining, never does.

### 🌫 Vanish: the potion cloud was never actually swept
- **The line that was supposed to empty it could not ever have worked.** `VanishManager` swept the synced effect-particle list with `p.getClass().getMethod("removeEffectParticles")` into a `catch (Throwable ignored)`. `Class.getMethod` returns **public** members only, and `LivingEntity.removeEffectParticles()` is `protected` - so it threw `NoSuchMethodException` on every tick of every vanish and reported nothing, and the same string lookup would have failed under production mappings too. It is an `@Invoker` now: resolved at build time, and an upstream rename is a compile error instead of a tell that survives a release.
- **Measured, not assumed.** The new check fills a control body's cloud and reads it back (`1` entry, so vanilla's own writer is really filling it), then vanishes a second body and reads it again - **`1` before this change, `0` after**. That is the reported "it still shows particles": the list *is* the cloud, because the client draws it from exactly this data and renders it at reduced frequency for an invisible body, and the list was full.
- **A second mixin was written for the other half and deliberately not shipped** - one that cancels vanilla's own writer for that list on a hidden body. It could not be shown to apply in the test harness, and a guard nobody can demonstrate is a claim, not a fix. The sweep runs every tick, so the half that is measurable is the half that went in.

### 📵 Vanish: a Bedrock client cannot be told to hide anything
- Every mute a vanish has is a **client-side** one - the sprint dust, the landing puff, the water splash and the boot steps are all drawn and played by whoever is looking, from state the server merely syncs. That is why the vanish is seamless on a client running this mod, and why it is not on one that cannot be patched at all: a Bedrock client arrives through Geyser, draws its own particles from the state it is sent, and is a plume of dust following an invisible moderator around.
- **The one mechanism that works on a client nobody can patch is to not send it a body.** While a moderator is vanished, the entity is removed from the worlds of **Bedrock viewers only**, on the same four-tick clock the tab list already uses so a re-track or a chunk re-send cannot quietly put it back - and handed over on return as a full pair of spawn **and** metadata packets, which is exactly what vanilla itself sends when a viewer starts tracking somebody. Sending the spawn alone is how a staff member comes back as a blank body, so both are sent. Java clients are untouched: a modded one already hides the body itself, and removing it there would be a flicker bought for nothing.
- This is the one change here that could not be tested against the real client - there is no Geyser in the build - so it is the one to watch: if a Bedrock player ever stops seeing a staff member after an unvanish, it is the re-add rather than the removal, and relogging that viewer restores them.

## 1.9.10

### 🔓 A dial could switch the kick off without saying so
- **The automatic half was only reachable from inside the alert branch.** `flag()` decided whether to tell staff, and `enforce()` was called from the branch that ran when it had; a finding that was over the enforcement bar but under the alert bar fell out of the method one branch earlier and was never offered to the failsafe at all. With `alert-vl` set above `enforce-vl` - a legal pair of values, and the pair an owner reaches for when the alerts are too chatty - that was a server with everything switched on, a failsafe that was on, and nobody ever removed. "The anticheat kick does not work" is exactly what that looks like from the outside, and nothing in any log said why. Deciding to act on a finding and deciding to speak about it are two different questions with two different dials, and they are asked separately now: every finding that reaches `flag()` is offered to `enforce()`, which keeps every one of its own gates, so the change can only act where the old path could. Pinned by `anticheat.the-failsafe-is-not-switched-off-by-the-alert-dial`, which raises the alert bar to its maximum and requires the enforcement rule to be unmoved, and by an audit rule that fails the build if `enforce(...)` ever returns to that branch.
- **A kick now says whether it cut anything.** The old disconnect did its work inside an `if` and swallowed everything else, so the three ways a removal fails - the body already gone, the connection already null, the call throwing - looked exactly like a successful kick to every caller, every log line and every test. That is the state in which "the kick does not work" cannot be argued with. Each outcome is named now, `/ff anticheat kick` reports which one happened instead of claiming success, and the connection that was actually closed is logged as closed.
- **And the ladder is written where the admin is already looking:** `/ff anticheat status` now prints the failsafe's real numbers - that it needs three enforcement-level findings at least a minute apart, that a body hot on three physical checks at once counts as one, and that it stands down entirely while any moderator is online. "I flew around for a minute and nothing happened" is the report that line answers, and every figure on it is read off the constants rather than typed.

### 🧠 Corroboration is evidence now - a quiet cheater is no longer invisible
- **Every bar in this module judged one model at a time, and nothing ever added them up.** A client that is careful with its speed, careful with its reach and careful with its flight sat under all three bars forever, which is the shape of every hacked client written by somebody who knows how an anticheat reads. The answer is not to lower a bar - lowering them is what removes honest players - so the evidence is allowed to be the **agreement**: three separate physical models hot at once, each at the level that would have alerted staff about it on their own, inside half a minute. Speed is the distance a body moved, flight is whether anything held it up, reach is where its arm ended, and three of those lit together is not a second an honest player has. Counted over `AUTO_ENFORCE` rather than over every check, because this is evidence about what a body *did*.
- **Reaching the automatic half without going through the alerts did not make it less careful.** The confidence discount - a finding taken while the server is missing its ticks, or on a connection that is far behind, is worth less - is now required on that road too, alongside the same two things that can stand in for it in the alert rule: enough repetition, or corroborating checks. Pinned by `anticheat.corroboration-is-what-catches-a-quiet-cheater`, which holds both ends of the rule (three agreeing act, two do not) and requires a finding the server itself discounted to below the confidence bar to still be refused.

### ⛏ Ore-vision is answered by a person again, not by a disconnect
- **It was the one entry in the auto-enforce list whose evidence is a habit rather than a physical impossibility.** A body that crossed more distance than any input produces, a body standing on nothing, a break that finished in fewer ticks than the block has - those are statements about what a body did, and a wrong one is a bug in a model. Ore-vision is three mining statistics: how much of what you broke was valuable, how little stone sat between two finds, how many ores came out of stone nobody had opened. An honest player produces every one of them - a straight line toward a seam, a lucky deep dive, a branch mine at diamond depth. It keeps its level, its alert and its ledger entry and no longer carries a kick behind it, and the build fails if it goes back on that list.

## 1.9.9

### 🌪 The Gale Warden fights without saying a word
- **He is silent now - all of him.** The summon banner, the line on nearly every move, the phase announcements and four taunts on a timer are all gone; nothing in his file sends a chat line at all. What is left is the action bar naming each move as it starts, which is the fight telling you what is about to happen rather than the boss narrating it. A boss that talks through its own moves is a boss you read instead of watch, and he was the loudest fight in the mod.
- **A chat sender cannot come back by accident:** `ffAuditSources` fails the build on `Chat.raw`, `announce`, `announceNear`, `speak` or `taunt` reappearing in `GaleWardenManager.java`, and the same rule pins `setNoGravity(false)` - he is a breeze, so a breeze that stops obeying gravity hovers, and a hovering boss reads as a hologram.

### 🌀 The sky gets a tornado, and a hurricane to end on
- **Tornado**, in the first phase: a column nine blocks tall that walks the arena on its own drift, dragging everyone inside it toward its eye and holding them off the floor. **Twin Twisters** in the second: two of them, with a gap between them worth finding. **Hurricane** is the finisher - a storm centred on him and travelling with him, thirty-four blocks wide, that pulls the whole arena inward and then lets go in one enormous blast. All three are drawn every tick of their life: the column, the floor ring and the spiral of gusts that says which way it is turning.

### 🌊 The Drowned Sovereign gets the whole sea
- **It is almost entirely water particles now** - splash, falling water, bubbles, drip, dolphin and squid ink, layered per move rather than one puff each - and the **Final Tsunami** closes the last phase: a wall across the width of the arena that leaves the ground it crossed standing in water.
- **The looping guardian noise is gone for good.** `Drowned Call` used to raise guardians one time in three, and a guardian's attack is a beam it keeps firing for as long as it can see you - so a fight left unquiet behind a player's back was a chorus of guardian attacks running off screen, forever, because those bodies were marked persistent and never left. The call raises drowned now, nothing persistent, and every one of them is on the fight's ledger so the fight takes them back down with it.
- **And the ones already standing in a world are swept.** Changing what the call raises fixes every future fight and does nothing about a guardian an older build left behind - it was spawned persistent, so it is a body in the save, and it will still be firing that beam next session. A periodic sweep now removes any body still carrying the call's minion tag, matched on the tag rather than the entity type so an ocean monument's own guardians are never touched, and skipping the crew of a fight that is still running (they carry the same tag, and a sweep that matched on the tag alone would quietly empty every `Drowned Call` five minutes in). The sea boss's own stray sweep was in the same state - written and never called - so a fight that ended in a crash left an immortal body in the world; both are scheduled now, and the build fails if either call goes missing or if a guardian is ever spawned here again.

### 🔇 One sound that was pulsing too fast to read as weather
- The hurricane's wind retriggered every ten ticks - a drone five times a second, which stops sounding like a storm and starts sounding like a stuck sound. It is every forty ticks, and the storm itself is unchanged.

## 1.9.8

### 🛡 A creative player is out of reach of every fight
- **The report was "the bosses can damage me when I first join the world as creative".** Every scripted move in this mod hurts a player by calling `hurtServer` directly, and the mod's own hooks run *ahead* of vanilla's invulnerability test - so a body that had just logged in, still having its abilities set, could be hit and killed by a fight it had never joined. It is answered once, in the shared boss layer (`BossEmpowerment.blocksCreative`), registered as the **first** damage rule in the pipeline: a melee swing, a skull, a beam, a wave, a scripted slam and a freeze are all refused, and the two things that belong to the player rather than to a fight - the void, and `/kill` - are left alone, because a creative player an admin cannot remove is a different bug wearing this one's coat.
- **And the paths that kill by hand are covered too**, because a rule in the damage pipeline never sees them: the Mirage Castle's collapse deadline and the Mindbinder's drain both check now. The drain asks about creative only - this mod puts a *held* player into spectator mode on purpose, so that their own camera watches the body being worn, and a rule that a camera move could switch off is not a rule.
- Pinned by `bosses.a-creative-player-is-out-of-reach`, which reads the decision off the damage sources themselves - `mobAttack` and `magic` refused, `genericKill` and `fellOutOfWorld` allowed - so the boundary is a number rather than an argument, and by the two-way pair in `deaths.corruption-kill-keeps-the-pack`: the same staged hold kills a player in survival and leaves a player in creative standing.

### 🌪 The Gale Chakram is a body in flight, not a puff of smoke
- **It carries a spinning block display now**, from the moment it leaves the hand to the moment it is caught - a thin disc, tipped onto its edge and turned twenty-six degrees a tick, moved by the same code that already owned where the ring was. The picture and the damage therefore cannot disagree: the ring hits exactly where the disc is drawn, which is what makes timing the return possible at all.
- A display entity rather than an item or a projectile, deliberately: it has no gravity to fight, no collision to push players with and no AI to steer it (see below). It is taken out of the world on every route the ring can leave on - the catch, a Reload, a Cyclone Return's last turn, the owner's death or disconnect - so nothing is left standing in the air.

### 🧟 The world answers a death by zombie
- **Die to a zombie's own hand and another one rises where you fell**, holding what you were holding and wearing one piece of your armour, picked from what you actually had on. It says nothing at all - no chat line, no death message of its own, no announcement: it is a reactive feature, not an event, so the player reads it off the world rather than off the chat box. It stays standing where it was left, and it never picks anything up.
- **It holds copies, and it hands nothing back.** The kit is captured in the death-drop hook, the one moment a player's inventory is still whole, and the duplicate is marked for life: it carries no loot table at all (enforced in the loot-table hook, not merely by drop chances), it is skipped for experience, and it cannot make a duplicate of itself - which is what a death snowballing through a crowd would be. Killing your own reflection is not a way to get a second copy of anything.
- Pinned by `reactive.a-duplicate-hands-nothing-back`, which reads a live zombie's loot table before and after it is marked (a vanilla zombie *has* one, so the check cannot pass for the wrong reason), and by the capture itself: the weapon and the armour piece a body would rise with are read back off the player they were taken from, and the kit is filed under that player and nobody else.

### 🎁 Every box pays in Common, Rare, Epic and Legendary now
- **The middle of the ladder had no shape.** A box had exactly two outcomes - a legendary, or "something else" - and every non-legendary prize came off one flat ladder, so the material that takes your set to Tier III and a stack of primarine shards felt identical to open. Each prize is *described* now: the band is written on the item above "Your prize from the Loot Box", the reveal is drawn at the size the band deserves (its ring, its flash colour and every shape of it climb from Common to Legendary), and the chat line names it.
- **The bands are a description, not a second table** - no odds moved, and that is pinned: the check walks every family's own legendaries and requires them to read as Legendary, requires the material each set is upgraded with to read as Epic (asked through the forge's own mapping, so a set that gains a material gains a band on the same line), and requires the reveal to actually get bigger with the band.
- **What did change is what a warm box pays.** The soft-pity draws used to be spent only when a legendary was not in hand, so a box that rolled diamonds four times paid exactly what a cold one paid - an invisible number the pity bar promised and nobody could feel. Every draw now compares bands and any step up is kept, so a warm box turns Commons into Rares and Rares into Epics on its way to the jackpot it was always chasing.

## 1.9.7

### 💥 Every boss in the mod had lost its VFX, from one missing line
- **`BossVfx.beginTick()` was never called.** The shared particle budget spends per player per tick and stops drawing once a player's share is gone - and nothing ever handed the share back, so the *first* effect of a session spent the floor and every boss effect after it, for the rest of that session, was dropped in silence. No exception, no log line, no failing test: from the player's side the bosses simply had no effects. It also ate the Mirage Castle's collapse, which had been routed through that same budget. The reset is wired into the server tick now, the ledger is asserted by a self-test, and the audit fails the build if the call ever goes missing again.
- This is very likely what "the Drowned Sovereign does not use any of its moves and has NO VFX" was: the moves *were* firing - the fight log shows him taunting, entering phase two and announcing BLACK TIDE - but they were drawing nothing.

### 🌊 The Drowned Sovereign
- **He no longer burns.** A drowned left in daylight catches fire, which turned the king of the deep into a running torch the moment the sun came up.
- **He wears what he is**: a golden crown and a trident, re-applied every tick (a player who stripped him would otherwise spend the rest of the fight as a bare mob), with no drop chance on either.
- **He walks.** Still no vanilla AI - every move is scripted - but he now stalks the nearest player at a speed under a player's walk, stops at a spacing so he is never inside the person he is about to hit, and never jumps.

### 🌪 The Gale Warden
- **He moves too, and still without a breeze's brain.** A vanilla breeze sprints and vaults on a whim, which reads as a pinball and makes a fight built on *momentum* impossible to read. His movement is written by hand: a slow stalk toward whoever is closest, held still through his own wind-ups so the tells stay readable.

### 🗣 The bosses stopped talking
- **Speech is off.** Both new bosses had a voice - a taunt every eleven seconds, a line for each phase, a quotation wrapped around most moves - and it all arrived in chat. Anything carrying a quotation mark is now dropped at the one funnel every line passes through. The move *tells* are not speech and are untouched: "BLACK TIDE - get out of the dark water" is how the move is read, so it stays.

### 📊 `/ff scoreboard off` works
- **It was a command-tree bug, not a board bug.** `on` and `off` both required a line name, so the command a player actually types - `/ff scoreboard off` - matched no executor at all and answered with a usage error. Standing alone they are now the whole-board switch (the same call `enable`/`disable` makes); `on <line>` and `off <line>` still flip a single line.

## 1.9.6

### 🌦 The event rotation no longer fires the moment a world loads
- **Every load rolled for an event on its first tick, so the event a player met was always the first draw of the session.** The cause was a saved tick *number*: a world that ended a session at tick 36000 wrote `"next_roll": 36000`, the next session's clock started again at zero, and that value came back either an hour away (with the old clamp, which is why events once looked impossible to spawn) or already due (with the clamp removed, which is this bug). The state is stored as ticks *remaining* now - a fact that is true in any session - and a load buys exactly one full interval of quiet: never sooner, and never a longer silence just because the last session ended mid-event.
- **An event no longer resumes across a load.** A world is not being played while it is closed, and resuming is how the same weather ends up waiting at every login. The leftover world state (a standing Mirage Castle, snow on the ground, a sky left raining) is still swept up on load, exactly as before.
- **The anti-repeat rule remembers four events instead of one.** A single slot only forbids a repeat *back to back*, so with twelve events and a roll every five minutes the identical shower could return well inside the hour - and a reload between the two draws laundered the repeat completely. The window is written to disk, so quitting cannot launder it either, and a file written before the window existed seeds it from `last_event` rather than handing everybody one free repeat.
- **What was *not* wrong, and how that was shown.** The weights are fair: 200,000 draws put the meteor shower at 7.47%, and the world's own timestamped backups show `double_trouble`, `xp_frenzy` and the shower all having run. The shower is simply the loudest event in the catalogue - it re-announces itself in chat every thirty seconds for ten minutes, with craters to match - and combined with an event that started the instant you loaded, it was the one that read as "always meteor and nothing else".

### ⚒ The two newest sets have their own forge material
- **This fixes a real bug: neither new set could be upgraded properly, and both were being upgraded by the wrong material.** `isForgeLegendary` had never learned the Sea and Gale sets, and the forge's accept test was a chain of per-set branches whose final `else` was an unconditional "Shattered Mind" - so a Leviathan's Grasp was upgraded by a *Mindbinder's* material while having no material of its own. Both sets are forge legendaries now, and each answers to exactly one material and refuses every other set's.
- **Two new materials: the Abyssal Pearl** (the Drowned Sovereign) and the **Gale Core** (the Gale Warden), each drawn by the same checked-in generator as the rest of its set, with its own model and pack definition. Tier I to Tier II costs one, Tier II to Tier III costs two - the same ladder every other set uses.
- **Each boss drops its own material instead of the shared Magical Essence** (which is not a material either set can be upgraded with), and each of their loot boxes pays it in its material band, so the gear you are wearing is fed by the fight you won it from.
- **The forge's accept test is one lookup now, not eight nested branches.** A set's material is decided in a single place (`upgradeMaterialFor`), and both the accept test and the "you need N of X" messages read it back - so a set can no longer be in the forge but missing from the list of what it takes, and adding a new one is a single line. The error messages now name the material from the item itself, because that second hand-written copy of the mapping is what drifted.

## 1.9.5

### 🌊 The Drowned Sovereign - the sea boss
- **A giant drowned, made giant by the `SCALE` attribute rather than by a different mob.** He is a drowned at 2.6x, and he is driven by his own script (no AI), because every one of his moves is about *where you are standing* and a mob that also throws its own attacks is a mob whose tells cannot be read.
- **Phase I - The Leviathan** (760 HP, down to 60%): Tidal Crash, Depth Charge, Abyssal Tentacles, Riptide, Undertow, Drowned Call, Pressure Crush, Tidal Prison. Almost nothing here is damage. A wave moves you backward, a whirlpool decides which ground is yours, a Depth Charge is telegraphed for two full seconds before it lands, and Tidal Prison decides for one player that they are not standing anywhere for three seconds. The counterplay is footing, not more hitting.
- **Phase II - The Abyss Opens** (60%): Maelstrom, Abyssal Beam, Leviathan Dive, Black Tide, Call of the Deep, Crushing Depths, Tsunami. The arena stops being safe - a dark beam across the water, water that hurts for six seconds, eyes underwater whose beams you have to walk around, and three enormous waves in sequence rather than one.
- **Phase III - Drowned God** (20%): Worldbreaker Tide, Abyssal Maw, Leviathan's Wrath, Dead Sea, Final Depth. He stops defending. A vortex drags everyone to the middle, natural regeneration is switched off, and every player is marked for a volley of delayed explosions that all land at slightly different times.
- **He dies on camera.** The killing blow is cancelled and replaced with a ceremony - the sea takes him back rather than letting the body fall over mid-move with a dozen hazards still in the air. The ceremony's own end is the only route that pays the loot, and a once-only ledger behind it means an admin's `/kill` still pays out instead of dropping nothing.

### 🌪 The Gale Warden - the sky boss
- **Not a warden. A giant breeze**, and giant by the `SCALE` attribute (2.4x) - the fight is meant to read as one enormous breeze, so it is one enormous breeze.
- **His gimmick is momentum, not wind.** He does not throw projectiles at you; he decides what your movement does. Momentum Theft banks what you spend and hands it back in one blast, Reversal turns knockback into pullback so the launch you started becomes the one that drags you in, Sky Launch throws you up and then side currents decide where you land, and Zero Point stops everything - you, the projectiles, the particles, him - for two seconds before releasing all of it at once.
- **Phase I** (760 HP, to 60%): Momentum Theft, Reversal, Windstep, Dead Air, Pressure Point, Gale Counter. **Phase II - The Broken Sky**: Sky Launch, Cyclone Dash, Momentum Swap, Falling Sky, Vacuum. **Phase III - THE GALE**: No Ground, Zero Point, Heaven's Drop, Four Winds, Absolute Momentum.
- **Gale Counter makes hitting him a decision.** While he is braced, a blow from a player is thrown back at whoever landed it, from the side they are standing on - so you cannot mindlessly spam-hit him while he winds up.
- **Absolute Momentum is a damage check, not a timer.** He charges, huge wind rings expanding around him, and the longer the charge runs the bigger the eventual launch. Landing enough damage inside the window interrupts it. Fail, and the whole arena eats one enormous wind blast - ridiculous on purpose, and deliberately not a one-shot.

### ⚔ Six legendaries, and each one is about a different question
- **The sea set is about where the other body ends up.** *Leviathan's Grasp* (a claw): Grasp hauls the nearest target in, Crush drives a target already inside reach into the floor, sneak opens an Undertow vortex at your feet, and jumping into a strike comes down in a water shockwave - with a passive that scales knockback by how *hurt* the target is, so a nearly-dead enemy is a body you launch across the room. *Tidecaller* (a trident) sends a rolling wave along your sightline that picks up everything it passes and throws it where it was going. *Abyssal Chain* controls distance instead of health: Chain Pull yanks a body to you, Abyssal Hook pulls **you** to a wall, sneak opens a vortex at the impact, Depth Mark makes everything you hit take far more knockback, and a blow landed straight out of a hook is a Deep Strike.
- **The sky set is about what is moving and how fast.** *Skybreaker* (a greatsword): Wind Slash throws a horizontal blade down your sightline, Updraft lifts you and the target on a landed hit, Downforce slams an airborne target into the ground, the passive pays more the faster you were already moving when you swung, and Break the Sky leaps and comes down in a radial wind shockwave. *Gale Chakram* is a returning throw that hits on the way out **and** on the way back, curves when thrown from a sneak, gives you a shove upward if you catch it airborne, ramps its knockback on consecutive hits against the same body, and occasionally circles you twice before it comes home. *Warden's Mantle* is the boss's own mobility and not a weapon at all: a quarter of a fall's damage, a sneak-double-tap dash, a sneak-jump updraft, a tailwind after a few seconds of sprinting, and one save per minute when a fall would have killed you.
- **The Mantle has no right-click on purpose.** A chestplate's right-click is how you put it on, so all four of its abilities are read off the tick loop - the same shape the Astral Mantle already uses - and it is declared passive rather than shipped as a dead click.

### 🎁 Three boxes each, and new art for all ten items
- **Both fights pay three loot boxes per kill, into the inventory of everyone who fought** - the same number and the same routing the older bosses use, including the overflow-into-`/claim loot` rule so a full inventory never eats a box. Each boss also rolls at one of its own three legendaries, because a fight you have to re-summon should be a reason to come back rather than a dump.
- **The two boxes are real families, not reskins.** Each has its own prize table (the sea box pays prismarine and sponge, the sky box pays feathers, phantom membranes and a Slow Falling potion), its own guaranteed-legendary picker, and its own persisted pity counter.
- **Ten new textures, drawn by a checked-in generator** (`tools/make_sea_and_sky_textures.py`) so the art is reproducible and reviewable rather than a binary blob with no provenance, plus the matching model files and the pack's `custom_model_data` definitions. Both tridents are the first entries this pack has ever put on the trident base item.

### 🏰 The Mirage Castle's collapse no longer floods a Bedrock client
- **The fade was sending an unbounded number of particles, per tick, to every player in the level.** The collapse is the heaviest effect in the mod on purpose - several sends a tick for a full minute, at counts chosen so the shape reads from the far side of the biome - and each send fans out to everyone. A Bedrock client arriving through Geyser turns every single particle into its own packet on a protocol with a fraction of Java's headroom, so the client is handed more than it can drain and stops answering. That is the report: *the fade spams particles and lags and crashes the other person.*
- **The castle now draws through the shared particle budget** that every boss effect already uses. Bedrock gets the same shape at a fifth of the count with its own per-tick ceiling, nobody is sent more than one tick's worth, and a Java client sees what it always saw. The guards dissolving into dust, the elite's payout and the collapse all route through it now, not only the collapse's explosion.

### 🔒 What proves it
- `drowned.sovereign-rules` and `gale.warden-rules` assert **both fights' phase tables literally** (all 8/7/5 and 6/5/5 named moves, per phase), that the move gap strictly shortens as the fight goes on - *"becomes extremely aggressive"* as a number rather than a promise - and that the parts that make the last phases work at all are real: the sea boss's telegraph, prison duration and Final Depth marks, and the sky boss's charge time, interrupt cost, counter stance and theft window.
- `sea-and-sky.the-legendaries-are-six-and-they-do-things` asserts the **shape** of every passive rather than one sampled value: the Grasp's knockback is monotone from full health to empty and at least double at the bottom, Momentum is exactly zero at a standstill, rises with speed and is capped, Razor Current ramps and then stops ramping, and Depth Mark multiplies a marked body without touching an unmarked one. It also proves the Mantle is recognised as worn and softens a fall *only* when worn, and that the four schedulers (wave, vortex, ring, sky-slam) empty on teardown - a list that only ever grows is a server that gets slower the longer it has been up.
- **And the wiring is pinned, because almost none of it is visible from outside.** A boss whose tick is not registered still spawns and still dies; a lethal-blow rule that is not registered still lets the body fall over; a loot box missing from the right-click gate opens for every other box and does nothing for that one. Each is now a build failure with the consequence spelled out.

## 1.9.4

### 📡 The address on the board is the address you are playing on
- **It was the one line the board had to be *told*, so it printed a hardcoded string.** An address is not about the player, and the server cannot look it up either: the host a client dials is sent once, in the handshake, before it has a name, a uuid or anything to file it under - and vanilla keeps none of it. Which is why the board shipped `play.fortuneandfavors.net`: the same address for every player, on every server, including servers that string had nothing to do with.
- **So it is read back now, per player, from the handshake that connection sent.** `HandshakeAddressMixin` records the host and port at the head of vanilla's handshake handler - the only moment they exist - and `ConnectionAccessor` ties that connection to the player who used it. Keyed by the connection rather than by the remote socket address, deliberately: two players behind one router share a socket address, and giving one player a board that names the other's server is the exact class of mistake the sidebar was rebuilt to stop. Each player now sees the address they actually joined through, which is also the only one guaranteed to work for them - two players can arrive through two hostnames and both be right.
- **The port comes with it when it isn't 25565, and a modded client's payload does not.** A handshake host carries the client's own data after a `NUL` (Fabric clients do; so does every BungeeCord-style forwarder), and printing that on a sidebar is not an address. It is dropped, the port is appended only when a bare address would be wrong, and the whole string is capped - it arrives from a client and ends up on screen.
- **Empty now means "the address you joined on".** The box on the board screen is an *override*, and emptying it gives the live address back rather than blanking the line. A board saved by an older build that stored the old hardcoded default is read as un-written rather than as the player's own text, so the real address reaches those boards too instead of being pinned forever.
- **And the address can never be the reason a sidebar is wide.** A sidebar is exactly as wide as its widest line, and the address is the one line a player neither wrote nor can shorten - so it is trimmed to the width of the longest *other* text on the board (the banner included, unless the banner *is* the address). Colour codes are not width, so an address written with them is not trimmed to nothing while looking short.
- If there is no address to print at all - nobody wrote one, this player never sent a handshake through this session, and the server binds no real host - the line draws **nothing**, rather than a blank row on every board that cannot know its own address.
- Pinned by `board.the-address-is-the-one-you-joined-on`, which checks what a handshake host turns into (NUL payload dropped, port kept when it is not the default, a carried port not doubled, a client-picked length capped), that a written address overrides the live one, that clearing it goes back to live, and that the address row is never wider than the lines around it. It also proves the plumbing is *live* rather than merely present: that `ConnectionAccessor` is applied to the packet listener and resolves the field it names (an accessor that is registered but unremapped compiles, applies, and then throws the first time a board asks for an address), and that loading the handshake listener actually merges the recording handler - a shadow that no longer matches its target or an injection point that has been renamed fails in the self-test rather than when the first player joins. The two new mixins are pinned as hooks as well, so dropping either from the mixin config fails the build with the consequence spelled out.

## 1.9.3

### 🔒 Only you can be shown your board, and the address line is off
- **The one route that could publish a board is now closed.** A display slot is *global*: vanilla broadcasts a slot's display packet to every player, then starts tracking whatever objective is in it and broadcasts every score written on it. So anything that ever puts a personal board's objective into one - an admin's `/scoreboard objectives setdisplay`, another mod walking the world's objectives, a future line of code here - hands the whole server that board and keeps it up. `show()` now looks at every slot on every refresh and takes the objective straight back out, which turns "only you see this" from a promise about which functions exist today into a property of the world.
- **The board is addressed to its owner, and that is now checked on the wire.** `board.only-you-are-ever-sent-it` runs two boards at once through the packet sink and asserts that every packet went to the player whose board it is, that the objective each client is told to draw is its own, and that no row or reset ever names another player's board - the failure mode being one board's stats drawn inside another player's sidebar.
- **The address line is off by default.** It was the widest line a fresh board could carry, and a sidebar is exactly as wide as its widest line - so a new board led with an address and read like a login screen with your balance under it. `/ff scoreboard ip <text>` writes the address **and** switches the line on, and promoting the address to the banner switches it on too, because a banner with no switch that brings it back is a banner you did not ask for.
- **Single player gets no board on a timer.** One player, no address, nobody to read it, and a refresh every second for decoration: the automatic refresh stands down there, so a board switched on for a server does not follow the same account into its own save. An explicit `/ff scoreboard` still draws one, which is what keeps the board editable offline - the rule is about what comes up by itself.
- **And the address cannot be turned on in single player at all.** There is no address to print in a world you opened, so the switch says no instead of storing a switch the board then refuses to honour: the line, `/ff scoreboard on ip`, and promoting the address to the banner all decline there and say why.
- Pinned by three new checks - `board.only-you-are-ever-sent-it`, `board.a-shared-slot-cannot-hold-it` (puts a board in the sidebar slot and watches it get pulled back out) and the address / single-player rules in `stats.scoreboard` - each of which was shown to bite by injecting the bug back, plus new audit rules that fail the build if the pull-back, the single-player rule or the address default is deleted.

## 1.9.2

### 📊 The sidebar now reaches the player it belongs to - because it was reaching nobody
- **A board that was turned on drew nothing, on any client, ever.** `/ff scoreboard` built a real objective on the world's scoreboard, sent a display packet naming it and wrote its ten lines as scores on it - and not one byte of it arrived. Vanilla sends an objective to a client only while that objective sits in a **global** display slot, which a personal board deliberately never does (a global slot is what would make every player read the same board), and a score is only ever broadcast for an objective being tracked that way - so the client was told to draw a board it had never been told about, which draws nothing at all. The objective and every row now go out on the owner's own connection: `ClientboundSetObjectivePacket(METHOD_ADD)`, then the display packet, then one score packet per row - and they come back off the same way, which is also what makes a board the server-wide gate has switched off stop being drawn.
- **A refresh that has nothing to say now says nothing.** The board used to re-derive all ten lines and re-send the display packet every second whether or not a number had moved - a packet per player per second for a sidebar that changes about once a minute - and a board whose *address* had been promoted to its banner went further: it compared a freshly built component against the live line while *creating* the objective from the title, so it tore the objective down and rebuilt it, resetting all ten of its rows, on every single refresh, forever. The objective is identified by the banner it was actually built with now, and each player's rows are remembered, so a refresh that finds nothing changed sends nothing at all.
- **Each row is keyed by its line, not by its own text.** An entry keyed by its text is a brand new entry the moment a balance moves - $1,000 to $1,001 was a reset and a fresh score last second - and one keyed by its position slides under every row below it the moment a line is switched off. Keys follow the line now, so a number moving *edits* a row, switching a line off costs one packet instead of ten, and the invisible marker the board used to prefix every line with (and every other reader of a board had to strip again) is gone.
- Pinned by `perf.the-sidebar-only-says-what-changed`, which drives the real paths for a real body and counts what actually goes out: turning the board on sends its objective first, then the display packet, then one packet per row; a second refresh sends nothing; a rename rebuilds once and then settles; promoting the address rebuilds once and then settles; switching a line off takes that row off and re-sends nothing else; and switching the board off leaves nothing behind on the world's scoreboard. The check was proved to bite both ways - re-sending the display packet every refresh failed it with *"a refresh with nothing to say pushed 12 packet(s) at one player"*, and dropping the objective packet failed it with *"the board was shown without sending its objective first"* - and four audit rules keep the three packets and the change check from being deleted quietly.

## 1.9.1

### 🩹 A totem pop does not end the session any more
- **The crash was not ours, and that is why it had to be handled here.** A totem pop - with a spare totem anywhere outside the hotbar - disconnected the client with **"Network Protocol Error"** in singleplayer as readily as anywhere else, from a `NullPointerException` inside the packet handler. The stack names exactly one culprit: `autototem` 1.1.0 re-stocks the popped totem by building its own `ServerboundContainerClickPacket`, and it passes a **literal `null`** where vanilla passes `ClientPacketListener.decoratedHashOpsGenenerator()`. Vanilla dereferences that generator once per component **on the client's own thread, while the packet is being built**, so the exception surfaces through `ClientPacketListener.handleEntityEvent` and the connection is dropped. There is no frame of this mod anywhere in the trace. (The bytecode is unambiguous - `aconst_null` immediately before the `HashedStack.create` call site.)
- **What can be fixed here is the fatality, not the mod.** `HashedPatchMap.create` now answers a missing generator with a map that has no components in it instead of letting the dereference happen: a hash is the server's *remote-slot* bookkeeping, and a map that does not line up makes the server resend that slot - a redundant update, where the alternative was a lost connection. Nothing vanilla reaches the branch, because nothing vanilla passes a null generator, and the guard ships **client-side only**, so a dedicated server is byte-for-byte what it was.
- The guard is pinned by `ffAuditSources` rather than left to trust: the build fails if `client/mixin/HashedPatchMapMixin.java` disappears, if it stops injecting at the head of `HashedPatchMap.create`, if it stops asking whether the generator is missing, or if it stops being listed in `fortuneandfavors.client.mixins.json` - the last of which is the wiring mistake that leaves a perfect guard as dead code with every self-test still green.
- If you would rather the offending mod were simply not there, disabling `autototem-1.1.0.jar` in the instance is the other half of the answer - 1.1.0 is the newest build for both 26.2 and 26.3, so the bug is upstream and unfixed.

## 1.9

### 🧩 The board answers the room, not the clock - and three of its situations had no bounty at all
- **Everything the reactive board knew about was something that *happened*.** A boss that killed a player, a kill spree, a player carrying the Bad Omen, a wither whose party had fallen - each is an event, reported once by the hook that saw it. Three states this server can sit in are not events, and the board was blind to all three: **a teammate who has just gone down while the rest of the party is still standing**, **a boss bar still on screen with nobody anywhere near the body**, and **a death drop nobody has come back for**. Each is now read off the world every pass rather than reported, and each carries its own bounty, because a rescue, a mop-up and a recovery are not the same work.
- **A teammate's fall is a call for backup, not a new bounty on a player.** When a mob drops somebody, the board hands the fight to whoever is still standing: `§cBackup: <name> is down` names the body that did it, asks for one to three of it, and pays for the mop-up. A *player* kill is deliberately not this - the hunt board already owns that, and a second bounty on somebody for one kill is a bounty on a happening the board already answered.
- **An abandoned boss bar is the loudest thing on the screen and the quietest thing on the board.** A fight that gets started and then walked away from leaves a bar that never leaves and no way for anyone else to take it. The sweep asks `BossManager` which of its fights currently have nobody within **48 blocks** of the body, waits **45 seconds** before believing it, and then posts `§cAbandoned: Slay the <boss>`.
- **And a grave that nobody came back for becomes anyone's to bring home** - after **ten minutes**, and only while its owner is offline, because while they are around the drop is theirs to walk back to. Claiming it pays the finder; the gear still goes to the person it belongs to, mailed if they are away. A job for a grave that has since been claimed, or whose owner has walked back, retires itself the moment it stops being true.
- Pinned by `contracts.a-party-that-fell-is-a-job` and by the three live-situation test hooks (`hasDownedJob`, `abandonedBosses`, `hasRecoveryJob`), which drive the real posting paths rather than a copy of them.

### 🗡️ The Warlord fights in phases, on his own clock, behind his own line
- **He used to be a vindicator with a name and three canned moves on the *level's* clock.** The slam fired on every 160th tick of the world's game time - so it landed whenever the metronome said, whether or not the Warlord was in the fight at all, and killing his entire escort changed nothing about him. A raid's finale was the same fight at every difficulty, and the one move that decides a room was instant.
- **The fight is a chain of command now, and the chain is what you have to break.** **Phase I, the Line:** while any of his escort stands the Warlord is *armoured by the room* (Resistance II) and his escort is hurried along - so a raid cannot be short-cut by ignoring the lieutenants and pouring everything into the one big health bar. **Phase II, the Warcry:** the moment the escort is gone he roars, gains speed and strength, and starts using his slam and his fire. **Phase III, the Last Stand:** below a third of his health, enraged, with a wider and faster slam, and two of his own guard called in - once.
- **And every slam is wound up in the open, long enough to walk out of.** The ring is painted on the floor with a heartbeat under it for the whole wind-up - **one second** in the last stand, **one and a half** in the warcry, and a full **two** in the line - so the answer is to leave the circle rather than to have been somewhere else. The slam is only ever wound up with somebody inside its reach, so an empty field cannot eat the fight's one telegraph - and the whole clock lives on the raid instead of the level, so the first slam is scheduled from the fight rather than from midnight.
- **And an abandoned raid now reaches the board.** The board's own abandoned-fight rule reads the bodies `BossManager` owns; a raid's Warlord is `PlayerRaidManager`'s, so a party that started a raid, died to it and logged off left a boss bar over a village and nothing to do about it. That is the same 48-block rule now, posted as `§cAbandoned: Raid Warlord` with the coordinates, and it retires the instant somebody picks the fight up - a fight that is being fought is not an abandoned one. The Warlord's death clears the job, too.
- Pinned by `raids.the-warlord-fights-in-phases` - the phase rule in both directions (an escort pins him at the line *whatever* his health; the last stand is only reachable once the escort is gone), a wind-up long enough to answer at every phase, a cooldown that always outlasts its own wind-up, a ring that stays a room rather than a map, and the ordering that makes the phases phases (the last stand strictly faster and wider than the warcry, and the line throwing no fire at all). The build audit fails if the Warlord stops being driven by his own clock, if the slam stops being wound up, if it stops asking whether anybody is in the ring, or if the phase stops being read off the escort and his health.

### ⛏️ Mining has a chain and a score, so the mine has a reason to move in
- **The mine paid exactly one thing: the ore in your hand, sold later.** The slab refills on its own clock right in front of a prisoner who never has to move, so "work the seams" was really "stand still and hold left-click" - flat work with a reward a menu away. Two mechanics answer that, and they are the same idea from both ends: **pay for *how* the ore is taken, not only for what it is.**
- **Momentum is the chain.** Ore-to-ore within **60 ticks** escalates a bonus on top of the haul - the first ore of a run pays nothing extra, the second pays 5%, and it caps at **+50%** - so standing still has a price and the reason to keep moving is the clock. The chain is shown on the action bar as it climbs, announced at every tenth link, and *dug rock never breaks it*: the stone between two veins is not part of the chain, because a chain that digging breaks is a chain nobody keeps.
- **A rich seam is the score.** A floor refill now occasionally drops a **3×3 pocket of the tier's own best material** into the wall - iron at the bottom, diamond at five, an ancient debris pocket at the top - with a glint of end rods so a prisoner working the wall can see it. Clearing the whole pocket pays a jackpot on top of the nine ore (climbing from **$250** to **$2,000**), with a warning line when one block is left, because the jackpot is the reason to look around: the ordinary wall does not have one, and the pocket does. One seam per prisoner at a time, dropped when they leave, so a shared slab never accumulates a wall of diamond on a timer.
- Pinned by `prison.momentum-pays-for-the-chain` (the bonus is monotonic, capped, and never more than half again; the chain grows on the edge of its own window and resets past it, and never grows on a clock that went backwards; an idle chain reads as expired) and `prison.a-rich-seam-is-worth-clearing` (nine blocks, a real chance of a seam rather than a wall of them, every tier's pocket cut from something better than that tier's ordinary ore and rock, and a jackpot that climbs without becoming a faucet). The audit fails if the mine stops calling the ore hook, if a refill stops planting a seam, or if an ore stops paying for the chain behind it.

### 🌳 The skill tree has a top, a capstone, and arithmetic that does not pay you twice
- **A finished tree kept paying.** A skill was uncapped: once all four - then six - trees were at 10 the system stopped, but until then every further ore still levelled a *maxed* Mining past ten and handed out a point for it. So the fastest way to finish the sixth tree was to stop feeding it, and the number every other system prices on - the sum of the six levels, which the job board and the scoreboard both read as `6 × MAX_LEVEL` - could be pushed past its own ceiling. **Levels are capped at 10 per tree**, a finished tree banks no more XP, and the tenth level now pays a **capstone point** on top of the usual one, so the end of a tree is the best single level in it rather than the quietest.
- **And the curve is a curve.** `25 + 45 × level` was flat in shape - level 10 asked for less than seven times what level 1 did, so a tree opened fast, stalled, and ended without ever feeling like a climb. A quadratic tail gives the late levels their weight (the tenth now costs **1,375 XP**), so a tree is something a player decides to finish.
- **Two refunds disagreed about arithmetic, and one of them paid twice.** The per-tree refund cleared the tree - which *is* what returns the points, since an unspent point is what you have earned minus what your trees hold - and then handed the level count back as fresh points on top, so buying a Cost-3 level and refunding it left a player richer than they started.
